Smooth 2-Group Extensions
and

Abstract
We study bundle gerbes on manifolds M M that carry an action of a connected Lie group G G . We show that these data give rise to a smooth 2-group extension of G G by the smooth 2-group of hermitean line bundles on M M . This 2-group extension classifies equivariant structures on the bundle gerbe, and its non-triviality poses an obstruction to the existence of equivariant structures. We present a new global approach to the parallel transport of a bundle gerbe with connection, and use it to give an alternative construction of this smooth 2-group extension in terms of a homotopy-coherent version of the associated bundle construction. We apply our results to give new descriptions of nonassociative magnetic translations in quantum mechanics and the Faddeev-Mickelsson-Shatashvili anomaly in quantum field theory. We also propose a definition of smooth string 2-group models within our geometric framework. Starting from a basic gerbe on a compact simply-connected Lie group G G , we prove that the smooth 2-group extensions of G G arising from our construction provide new models for the string group of G G .



Hamburger Beiträge zur Mathematik Nr. 834

ZMP–HH/20–10

EMPG–20–08

Contents

1
Introduction

This paper is motivated by the following problem from physics: In
[
BMS19
]
we showed how a bundle gerbe with connection on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
gives rise to a 3-cocycle on the translation group
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Even though this 3-cocycle is trivial in group cohomology, it is very interesting from a physical as well as from a mathematical perspective:
it gives a geometric explanation to the presence of nonassociativity
in quantum mechanics with magnetic monopole backgrounds, and it
implements the action of the parallel transport of a bundle gerbe on
its 2-Hilbert space of sections. This appearence of nonassociativity in
quantum mechanics goes back to
[
Jac85
,
GZ86
]
,
but as of yet the more natural extension to realistic scenarios
involving periodically confined motion on configuration spaces such as tori

{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
has not been worked out. The discussion
of
[
Jac85
]
was a response to the observed violation
of the Jacobi identity for the algebra of field operators in quantum gauge theories with
chiral fermions
[
Jo85
]
, which is a manifestation of the chiral anomaly. Interest in these
models has been recently revived through their conjectural relevance
to non-geometric flux compactifications of string theory,
which is based on backgrounds that are tori or more
generally torus
bundles
[
Lüs10
,
MSS12
,
BL14
,
MSS14
]
. However,
the original finding
[
BP11
]
of
nonassociativity in Wess-Zumino-Witten models based on other compact
Lie groups has so far received considerably less attention, and in
particular has not been understood from a geometric perspective.

In the present paper we work out the geometric framework and origin behind these results in complete generality.
Subsequently, we present several applications of our results in both
physics and mathematics, along the lines discussed above.
We consider an action
\Phiit\colon G\times M\longrightarrow M
of a connected Lie group
G
on a manifold
M
, where
M
is endowed with a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
.
One can now ask whether
\mathcal{G}
admits a
G
-equivariant structure.
At the very least, such a structure should consist of a choice of 1-isomorphism
\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}\mathcal{G}
for every
g\in G
.
Instead of considering possible choices for such 1-isomorphisms individually, we assign to
g
the groupoid of all such 1-isomorphisms.
This yields an object which can be understood as a bundle
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow G
of groupoids over
G
.
Considering
g=e
, the identity element of
G
, we see that its typical fibre is the groupoid
\mathrm{HLBdl}(M)
of hermitean line bundles on
M
.

The definition of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
so far does not capture the smooth structure of the gerbe
\mathcal{G}
.
We thus enhance the construction to take into account smooth families of elements of
G
.
Then one can make sense of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
as a category fibred in groupoids over a base category
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
that encodes smooth families of geometric objects.
Categories fibred in groupoids over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
assemble into a 2-category
\mathscr{H}
, and there exists a fully faithful inclusion of the category of smooth manifolds into
\mathscr{H}
.
Motivated by
[
SP11
]
we define a smooth 2-group to be a group object in
\mathscr{H}
.
One of the central examples for us is the smooth 2-group
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
of hermitean line bundles on
M
.
We introduce a notion of smooth principal 2-bundle in
\mathscr{H}
that lies between the definitions of higher principal bundles used in
[
SP11
]
and
[
NSS15
]
(see in particular Appendix
A.2
).
We show that our principal 2-bundles are well behaved from a homotopical as well as from a geometric point of view (more precisely, they form effective epimorphisms while also admitting local sections).
With the notion of smooth 2-group and principal 2-bundles, we can make precise what it means to be a (central) extension of smooth 2-groups in analogy to extensions of Lie groups.
Then, our first main results can be summarised as

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group acting on a manifold
M
, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a bundle gerbe on
M
.
Then:

(1)

There is a (non-central) extension of smooth 2-groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\ ,}

(1.2)

where
\underline{G}\in\mathscr{H}
denotes the category fibred in groupoids associated to
G
.

(2)

The smooth 2-group
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
acts on
\mathcal{G}
, and the action covers that of
G
on
M
.

(3)

The gerbe
\mathcal{G}
admits a
G
-equivariant structure if and only if there exists a morphism
\underline{G}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
of smooth 2-groups which splits the extension (
1.2
).

An extension similar to (
1.2
) was considered
in
[
FRS16
]
, where symmetries of a gerbe with connection were investigated in relation with higher geometric prequantisation.
Infinitesimal versions of the extension (
1.2
) were considered in
[
Col11
,
FRS16
]
, where it was shown that these give rise to the standard
H
-twisted Courant algebroid on
M
, where
H
is the 3-form curvature of the connection on
\mathcal{G}
.
These considerations have been expanded on and applied to higher versions of Kaluza-Klein reductions of string theory in
[
Alf20
]
.

Our point here is that in many applications, such as nonassociativity
in quantum mechanics and string theory, anomalies in quantum field theory, as well as interesting topological constructions, connections on
\mathcal{G}
only play a secondary role: in this context, they can be seen as a tool to compute the extensions (
1.2
) and their associated cocycles.
The key to this computability is an alternative presentation of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
in terms of a categorified descent construction.

In order to work out this construction, we introduce a novel global approach to the parallel transport of a bundle gerbe.
Parallel transport for gerbes has been constructed in
[
SW11
,
SW09
,
SW17
]
, but for our purposes a global, rather than local, treatment is necessary.
Our construction relies heavily on the transgression-regression
machine for bundle gerbes
[
Wal16
]
together with the properties of the fusion product and the connection on the transgression line bundle that were studied in
[
Wal16
,
BW18
]
.
Given a connection on
\mathcal{G}
, we construct its parallel transport as a quadruple
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}=({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star},\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}})
, consisting of the following data:
first, there is a 1-isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}
over the path space
PM
of
M
, where
{\mathrm{ev}}_{t}\colon PM\longrightarrow M
is the evaluation of a path at
t\in[0,1]
.
Second, there is a 2-isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}\colon{\mathtt{pt}}_{1|\gamma_{0}}^{\mathcal{G}}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{pt}}_{1|\gamma_{1}}^{\mathcal{G}}
for every smooth homotopy with fixed endpoints between paths
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, which depends smoothly on the paths and the homotopy.
The 2-isomorphisms
{\mathtt{pt}}_{\star}^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}}
implement the
compatibility of the parallel transport with concatenation of paths
and with constant paths, respectively.
Furthermore, the collection
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}
is required to be invariant
under thin homotopies in a precise way. We show

Theorem 1.3
.

Every bundle gerbe with connection has a canonical parallel
transport.

Using the parallel transport, we are able to write down a

\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
-valued Čech 1-cocycle on the covering of
G
by its
space of based paths.
These data are equivalently transition functions for an

\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
-principal 2-bundle
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}\longrightarrow\underline{G}\,
.
We construct
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
explicitly by a homotopy-coherent version of the associated bundle construction.
Then we prove

Theorem 1.4
.

The principal 2-bundle
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}\longrightarrow\underline{G}
is a smooth 2-group extension of
\underline{G}
by
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
.
There is a weakly commutative diagram of smooth 2-groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psiit}

(1.5)

The morphism
\Psiit
is an equivalence.

In the case
M=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
G=\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}
is the translation
group of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and where
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{I}_{B}
is a trivial gerbe on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a connection
B\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
corresponding to a
magnetic field, we show that the
extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}}(\mathcal{I})\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\,{\mathtt{t}}}
reproduces the 3-cocycles we obtained in
[
BMS19
]
.
We achieve this by choosing a certain global section of the path
fibration of
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\tt t}
and implicitly pass through
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
in the computation.
We show that the parallel transport we defined implements
nonassociative magnetic translations on the sections of the gerbe,
whereas the 2-group extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}}(\mathcal{I})\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\,{\mathtt{t}}}
allows us to understand the algebraic
structure of nonassociative magnetic translations even without making any reference to sections.
The latter is particularly useful in cases where there is no good notion of sections, such as when the Dixmier-Douady class of
\mathcal{G}
is non-torsion.
In particular, we study in detail the action of nonassociative
magnetic translations on tori
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
and give an explicit
description of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}}(\mathcal{G})
for general choice of a gerbe
\mathcal{G}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.

As a further application, we show that if
{\Gamma}
is a group of gauge transformations, the smooth 2-group extensions
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\underline{{\Gamma}}
control the Faddeev-Mickelsson-Shatashvili anomalies in quantum field theory
[
Fad84
,
FS85
,
Mic85
]
.
The relation between gerbes and these anomalies has been investigated in
[
CM95
,
CM96
]
, but only as algebraic objects, disregarding the smooth structures.
The relevant bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
lives on the space
{\mathscr{A}}
of gauge fields
and describes the obstruction to a Fock bundle descending to the orbit
space
{\mathscr{A}}/{\Gamma}
.
Here the extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\underline{{\Gamma}}
is split, so that
\mathcal{G}
admits an equivariant structure.
At the same time
\mathcal{G}
is trivialisable as a bundle gerbe, but the anomaly is precisely the obstruction to choosing a
{\Gamma}
-equivariant trivialisation.
This allows us to understand the anomaly in a conceptual way as a higher smooth 1-cocycle on
{\Gamma}
.

Finally, we consider the situation where
M=G
is a compact simply-connected Lie group, acting on itself by left multiplication, and where
\mathcal{G}
is a bundle gerbe on
G
whose Dixmier-Douady class generates
\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
We motivate and propose a new smooth string 2-group model for the string group of
G
.
For this, we first show that with our definition of principal 2-bundle, principal
{\mathsf{A}}
-bundles on a manifold give rise to
{\mathsf{A}}
-valued Čech 1-cocycles, for any smooth 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
.
Then we call a smooth 2-group extension
{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow\underline{G}
a smooth 2-group model for the string group of
G
if
{\mathsf{A}}
is equivalent to an Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(\mathbb{Z};2)
in a certain sense and the class in
{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{1}(G;{\mathsf{B}}{\operatorname{U}}(1))\cong\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})
extracted from the 2-bundle
{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow\underline{G}
is a generator.
Using this definition of smooth string 2-group models, we show

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
be the smooth 2-group extensions of
\underline{G}
by
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{G}
constructed from
\mathcal{G}
with respect to the left action of
G
on itself via left multiplication.
Then both
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
are smooth 2-group models for the string group of
G
.

The remainder of this paper is organised as follows.
In Section
2
we briefly recall some background material on diffeological spaces, bundle gerbes, and transgression.
Section
3
provides a motivation of the later
constructions on the level of principal bundles; many concepts become clear already at this level.
In Section
4
we provide our definition and construction of the parallel transport associated to a bundle gerbe with connection.
The construction of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
takes place in Section
5
.
Here we first motivate and then introduce the necessary language of Grothendieck fibrations, smooth 2-groups, and principal 2-bundles, before defining and studying the extensions
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
.
We conclude this section by relating these extensions to equivariant structures on
\mathcal{G}
.
In the remaining three sections we apply our general results:
in Section
6
we study nonassociative magnetic translations using our parallel transport, Section
7
contains the discussion of chiral anomalies and the Faddeev-Mickelsson-Shatashvili anomaly, and in Section
8
we show that
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
provide new models for the string group.
We defer some technical results on categories fibred in groupoids and on principal 2-bundles to Appendix
A
.

2
Preliminaries on diffeological spaces and gerbes

In this section we review some of the relevant background material related
to diffeological spaces and bundle gerbes that will be used throughout
this paper.

2.1
Diffeological spaces

Throughout this paper we will use diffeological spaces (see
[
IZ13
]
for an extensive
introduction) to describe
the smooth structure on infinite-dimensional spaces
such as path and mapping spaces. The idea behind diffeological spaces
is to describe the smooth
structure on a space
X
by specifying the set of smooth maps from Cartesian spaces to
X
. A
Cartesian space

c
is a smooth manifold diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We denote by
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
the category with
Cartesian spaces as objects and smooth maps as morphisms.

Definition 2.1
.

A
diffeological space
is a set
X
together with a collection of
maps
c\longrightarrow X
from Cartesian spaces into
X
, called
plots
, such that

(1)

the composition of a plot with a smooth map between Cartesian spaces is again a plot,

(2)

every map
\mathbb{R}^{0}\longrightarrow X
is a plot, and

(3)

if
f\colon c\longrightarrow X
is a map such that there
exists an open cover
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
c
by Cartesian spaces and
f_{|c_{i}}

is a plot for all
i\in I
, then
f
is a plot.

A map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
between diffeological spaces
is
smooth
if it maps plots of
X
to plots of
Y
.
We denote by
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}fg}
the category of diffeological spaces and smooth maps.
Isomorphisms in
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}fg}
are
diffeomorphisms
.

Remark 2.2
.

Usually plots are defined to be maps from open subsets
U
of Cartesian spaces to
X
.
Since every open subset
U
of a Cartesian space can be covered by Cartesian spaces, both definitions
are equivalent.
Diffeological spaces are exactly the concrete sheaves on
the site of Cartesian spaces
[
BH11
]
. This
implies that the category of diffeological spaces
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}fg}
admits all limits
and colimits, and is Cartesian closed. For more background on this
perspective on diffeological spaces, see also
[
Bun20a
]
.

\vartriangleleft

Important examples of diffeological spaces include the following.

Example 2.3
.

Every manifold
M
(possibly with boundaries or corners) defines a diffeological space by declaring
a map
f\colon c\longrightarrow M
to be a plot if and only if
f
is
a smooth map of differentiable manifolds. This defines
a fully faithful embedding of the category of smooth manifolds
\operatorname{\mathscr{M}fd}
into the
category of diffeological spaces
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}fg}
.
\vartriangleleft

Example 2.4
.

Let
X
be a diffeological space and
Y\subset X
a subset.
We can equip
Y
with a diffeology by declaring a map

c\longrightarrow Y
to be a plot if and only if the composition
with the embedding
Y\longrightarrow X
is a plot. This is called the
subspace diffeology on
Y
.
\vartriangleleft

Example 2.5
.

Let
X
and
Y
be diffeological spaces. The Cartesian product

X\times Y
is a diffeological space by declaring a map

f\colon c\longrightarrow X\times Y
to be a plot if and only if

\operatorname{pr}_{X}\circ f
and
\operatorname{pr}_{Y}\circ f
are plots, where
\operatorname{pr}_{X}
and

\operatorname{pr}_{Y}
are the respective projections of
X\times Y
onto
X
and

Y
. This is called the product diffeology on
X\times Y
.
\vartriangleleft

Example 2.6
.

Let
X
and
Y
be diffeological spaces. The set of smooth maps

Y^{X}
from
X
to
Y
becomes a diffeological space by declaring
a map
f\colon c\longrightarrow Y^{X}
to be a plot if and only
if the map

\displaystyle f^{\dashv}\colon c\times X

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Y

(2.7)

\displaystyle(u,x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto f(u)(x)

(2.8)

is smooth. This is called the mapping space diffeology on
Y^{X}
.
\vartriangleleft

A smooth map
f\colon M\longrightarrow M^{\prime}
between smooth manifolds is a surjective submersion if and only
if it admits local sections through every point in
M
, i.e. for every point
y\in M
there exists an open neighbourhood
U
of
f(y)
in
M^{\prime}
and a smooth map
\widehat{s}\colon U\longrightarrow M
such
that
f\circ\widehat{s}=1_{U}
is the identity map of
U
.
Surjective submersions define a Grothendieck topology on the category of manifolds, and many (higher) geometric objects on manifolds can be constructed via sheafification with respect to this topology (see, for instance,
[
NS11
]
).
On the category of diffeological spaces, a useful Grothendieck topology is induced by the subductions:

Definition 2.9
.

A smooth map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of diffeological
spaces is a
subduction
if for all plots
\varphi\colon c\longrightarrow Y

and
x\in c
there exists an open neighbourhood
U_{x}\subset c
of

x
and a plot
\widehat{\varphi}_{x}\colon U_{x}\longrightarrow X
such
that
\varphi_{|U_{x}}=f\circ\widehat{\varphi}_{x}
.

Example 2.10
.

Let
M
be a connected manifold. The space of paths in
M
with
sitting instants
PM
is the subspace of
M^{[0,1]}
of maps which
are constant in an open neighbourhood of
0
and
1
, equipped with
the subspace diffeology. The evaluation maps
{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}\colon PM\longrightarrow M

and
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}\colon PM\longrightarrow M
at
0
and
1
, respectively, are
subductions.

\vartriangleleft

Another source for subductions are quotient maps. Let
X
be a diffeological
space and
\sim
an equivalence relation on
X
. Then the space
X/{\sim}

becomes a diffeological space in a canonical way making the map
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow X/{\sim}
into
a subduction: a map
\varphi\colon c\longrightarrow X/{\sim}
is a plot if and only
if for all
x\in c
there exists an open neighbourhood
U_{x}\subset c
of

x
and a plot
\widehat{\varphi}_{x}\colon U_{x}\longrightarrow X
such
that
\varphi_{|U_{x}}=\pi\circ\widehat{\varphi}_{x}
. Clearly all subductions are of this type for
appropriate equivalence relations.
Diffeological quotients behave nicely with respect to quotients of manifolds when they exist.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
M
be a manifold with a free and proper action of a Lie group
G
.
Define an equivalence relation
\sim_{G}
on
M
by
m_{1}\sim_{G}m_{2}
if and only if
there exists
g\in G
such that
g\cdot m_{1}=m_{2}
.
Then the manifold
M/G
and the diffeological space
M/{\sim}_{G}
agree.

Proof.

From
[
Lee13
, Theorem 21.10]
it follows that
\pi\colon M\longrightarrow M/G
is
a surjective submersion. Since every surjective submersion is a subduction, the
statement follows.
∎

Definition 2.12
.

Let
X
be a diffeological space and
k\geq 0
. A
k
-form
\omega
on
X
consists
of a family of differential forms
\omega_{\varphi}\in\Omega^{k}(c)
indexed by the plots

\varphi\colon c\longrightarrow X
of
X
such that
\omega_{\varphi_{1}}=f^{*}\omega_{\varphi_{2}}

for all commuting triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}

(2.13)

Definition 2.14

(
[
Wal12b
, Section 3]
)
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
X
a diffeological space. A
principal
G
-bundle
on

X
consists of a subduction
\pi\colon P\longrightarrow X
together with a fibre-preserving
right action
P\times G\longrightarrow P
such that the map

\displaystyle P\times G

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P\times_{X}P

(2.15)

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(p,p\cdot g)

(2.16)

is a diffeomorphism.
A
connection
on a principal
G
-bundle
P
is a 1-form

A\in\Omega^{1}(P;\mathfrak{g})
satisfying

\displaystyle\rho^{*}A={\mathrm{Ad}}^{-1}_{\operatorname{pr}_{G}}(\operatorname{pr}_{P}^{*}A)+\operatorname{pr}_{G}^{*}\theta

(2.17)

on
P\times G
, where
\rho\colon P\times G\longrightarrow P
is the right
G
-action,

\theta
is the left-invariant Maurer-Cartan 1-form on
G
, and

\operatorname{pr}_{P}\colon P\times G\longrightarrow P
and
\operatorname{pr}_{G}\colon P\times G\longrightarrow G
are
the projections onto
P
and
G
, respectively.

2.2
Bundle gerbes and transgression

Bundle gerbes are higher categorical analogues of line bundles.
They provide a geometric realisation for the third cohomology group
with integer coefficients. Similarly to line bundles, bundle gerbes can be equipped with connections.
We briefly recall the definition of the 2-groupoid of bundle gerbes and their transgression to loop space.
For details we refer to
[
Wal07b
,
Wal16
,
Bun17
,
Mur96
]
.

Let
X
be a diffeological space.
We denote by
\mathrm{HLBdl}(X)
(resp.
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{\nabla}(X)
) the category of
hermitean line bundles (resp. with connection) on
X
.
Before defining bundle gerbes we need to introduce some notation:
for a subduction
\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow X
of diffeological spaces
we denote by

\displaystyle Y^{[n]}=\big\{(y_{0},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})\in Y^{\times n}\ \big|\ \pi(y_{0})=\pi(y_{1})=\dots=\pi(y_{n-1})\big\}\subset Y^{\times n}

(2.18)

the
n
-fold iterated fibre product
Y^{[n]}=Y\times_{X}\cdots\times_{X}Y
over
X
equipped with the subspace diffeology.
Then
Y^{[\bullet]}
is a simplicial diffeological space corresponding
to the subduction groupoid
Y\times_{X}Y\rightrightarrows Y
, and for

k<n
and

0\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n
we define the smooth face maps

\displaystyle\pi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\colon Y^{[n]}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Y^{[k]}

(2.19)

\displaystyle(y_{0},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(y_{i_{1}},\dots,y_{i_{k}})\ .

(2.20)

Definition 2.21

(
[
Wal16
]
)
.

Let
X
be a diffeological space. A
hermitean bundle gerbe
on
X
consists of a subduction
\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow X
, a hermitean line bundle
L\longrightarrow Y^{[2]}
, and a unitary isomorphism

\mu\colon\pi_{1,2}^{*}L\otimes\pi_{0,1}^{*}L\longrightarrow\pi_{0,2}^{*}L
of line bundles
over
Y^{[3]}
, called the
bundle gerbe multiplication
, which
is associative over
Y^{[4]}
,
i.e.
\pi_{0,2,3}^{*}\mu\circ(\pi_{0,1,2}^{*}\mu\otimes 1)=\pi_{0,1,3}^{*}\mu\circ(1\otimes\pi_{1,2,3}^{*}\mu)
.

A
connection
on a hermitean bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}=(\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow X,L,\mu)

consists of a hermitean connection
\nabla^{L}
on
L
and a 2-form
B\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
such that

(1)

the isomorphism
\mu\colon\pi_{1,2}^{*}L\otimes\pi_{0,1}^{*}L\longrightarrow\pi_{0,2}^{*}L

is parallel with respect to
\nabla^{L}
, and

(2)

the curvature of
\nabla^{L}
is equal to
\mathrm{i}\,(\pi_{1}^{*}B-\pi_{0}^{*}B)
.

The 2-form
B
is called a
curving
. The second condition
implies that the closed 3-form
\mathrm{d}B=\pi^{*}H

descends to a unique closed 3-form
H
on
X
with integer periods, which is called the
curvature
of the bundle gerbe
connection
(\nabla^{L},B)
.

Schematically, the data corresponding to a bundle gerbe can be
visualised by the
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,2}^{*}L\otimes\pi_{0,1}^{*}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{*}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{[3]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(2.22)

illustrating that hermitean bundle gerbes are equivalent to

{\operatorname{U}}(1)
-central extensions of subduction groupoids.

Example 2.23
.

Let
X
be a diffeological space. The
trivial hermitean bundle gerbe
\mathcal{I}
on
X
consists of the identity
subduction
1_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow X
together with the trivial hermitean line bundle

I\coloneqq X\times\mathbb{C}
over
X^{[2]}=X
and bundle gerbe multiplication

\displaystyle X\times(\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C}

(2.24)

\displaystyle\big(x,(z_{1}\otimes z_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(x,z_{1}\,z_{2})\ .

(2.25)

For every 2-form
B\in\Omega^{2}(X)
we can define a connection on

\mathcal{I}
by setting
\nabla^{I}=\mathrm{d}

and taking
B
as the curving. We denote the resulting hermitean bundle gerbe with connection
by
\mathcal{I}_{B}
. The curvature of
\mathcal{I}_{B}
is given by
H=\mathrm{d}B
.

\vartriangleleft

Hermitean bundle gerbes (resp. with connection) on a diffeological space
X
are the objects of a
symmetric monoidal bicategory
which we denote by
\mathrm{BGrb}(X)
(resp.
\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(X)
)
[
Wal07b
]
.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow X,L,\mu,\nabla^{L},B)
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow X,L^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\nabla^{L^{\prime}},B^{\prime})
be hermitean bundle gerbes with connection on
a diffeological space
X
. A
1-isomorphism

\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of hermitean bundle
gerbes (with connection) consists
of a subduction
\xi\colon Z\longrightarrow Y\times_{X}Y^{\prime}
, a hermitean line bundle
E
(with hermitean connection

\nabla^{E}
) on
Z
and (parallel) unitary isomorphisms

\alpha\colon\big((\operatorname{pr}_{Y}\circ\,\xi)^{[2]}\big)^{*}L\otimes\xi_{1}^{*}E\longrightarrow\xi_{0}^{*}E\otimes\big((\operatorname{pr}_{Y^{\prime}}\circ\,\xi)^{[2]}\big)^{*}L^{\prime}

over
Z^{[2]}
satisfying a natural set of compatibility
conditions, see
[
Wal16
]
for details. We will
denote such a 1-isomorphism by
(E,\xi)
(resp.
(E,\xi,\nabla^{E})
),
or sometimes simply by
E
.

Remark 2.27
.

One can also define non-invertible 1-morphisms of bundle gerbes by
using higher rank hermitean vector bundles
E
in Definition
2.26

[
Wal07b
]
.
In that case, a 1-morphism is weakly invertible if and only if the underlying hermitean
vector bundle
E
is of rank

1

[
Wal07a
, Proposition 2.3.4]
. However, with the exception of
Section
6
, we will only consider
invertible 1-morphisms of bundle gerbes in the
present paper.

\vartriangleleft

Definition 2.28
.

Let
(\xi_{a}\colon Z_{a}\longrightarrow Y\times_{X}Y^{\prime},E_{a},\nabla^{E_{a}},\alpha_{a})
and

(\xi_{b}\colon Z_{b}\longrightarrow Y\times_{X}Y^{\prime},E_{b},\nabla^{E_{b}},\alpha_{b})
be
1-isomorphisms
\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of hermitean
bundle gerbes with connection.
A
2-isomorphism
of bundle gerbes is an equivalence class of a subduction

\omega\colon W\longrightarrow Z_{a}\times_{Y\times_{X}Y^{\prime}}Z_{b}
and a parallel unitary
isomorphism
(\operatorname{pr}_{Z_{a}}\circ\,\omega)^{*}E_{a}\longrightarrow(\operatorname{pr}_{Z_{b}}\circ\,\omega)^{*}E_{b}

satisfying a natural compatibility condition, see e.g.
[
Wal07b
]
for details and the equivalence
relation.

Bundle gerbes on a diffeological space
X
are classified by their
Dixmier-Douady class in
\mathrm{H}^{3}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, analogously to the Chern-Weil
classification of line bundles by their Chern class in

\mathrm{H}^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
. For a bundle gerbe with connection, the Dixmier-Douady
class maps to the de Rham cohomology class of the curvature under
the homomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{3}(X;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}(X;\mathbb{R})
induced by
the inclusion of coefficient groups
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a hermitean bundle gerbe defined over a subduction
\pi:Y\longrightarrow X
, with underlying
hermitean line bundle
L\longrightarrow Y^{[2]}
.
Let
{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}
be an endomorphism of
\mathcal{G}
, with underlying hermitean vector bundle
A
over some subduction
\xi:Z\longrightarrow Y^{[2]}
.
Consider the hermitean vector bundle
L^{\vee}\otimes A
on
Z
, where
we denote the dual line bundle by
L^{\vee}
.
This comes with a canonical descent isomorphism defined by the diagram
[
Wal07b
,
Bun17
]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{1}^{*}(L^{\vee}\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2,3}^{*}L^{\vee}\otimes\xi_{1}^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{*}L\otimes\pi_{0,3}^{*}L^{\vee}\otimes\xi_{1}^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{0}^{*}(A\otimes L^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,3}^{*}L^{\vee}\otimes\xi_{0}^{*}A\otimes\pi_{1,3}^{*}L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2,3}^{*}\mu^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,1,3}^{*}\mu}

(2.29)

In fact, this construction establishes an equivalence of categories
\mathsf{R}\colon\mathrm{BGrb}(X)(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\mathrm{HLBdl}(X)
.

From a hermitean bundle gerbe with connection
\mathcal{G}
on a
diffeological space
X
we can
construct the
transgression line bundle

\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
over the loop space
LX

of
X
. The fibre
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}
over a loop
\gamma\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow X
consists of
equivalence classes
[[\mathcal{S}],z]
of a 2-isomorphism class of a trivialisation
\mathcal{S}\colon\gamma^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{0}
in
\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
over the unit circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
and an element
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Two pairs

([\mathcal{S}],z)
and
([\mathcal{S}^{\prime}],z^{\prime})
are equivalent if and only if
z^{\prime}=\operatorname{hol}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathsf{R}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{S}^{-1}))\,z
.
For the construction of a diffeological structure on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\coprod_{\gamma\in LX}\,\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}
we refer to
[
Wal16
]
.
A connection on a line bundle over the loop space
LX
is

superficial
if the holonomy around every thin
loop
1
1

1

A loop
\Gamma\in LLX
is
thin
if the adjoint map

\Gamma^{\dashv}\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow X
has
at most rank 1.
is equal to
1
and thin homotopic loops
2
2

2

Two loops

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\in LLX
are
thin homotopic
if there exists
a homotopy
h\in PLLX
such that the adjoint map
h^{\dashv}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow X
has at most rank 2.
have the same holonomy.
In the situation where
X=M
is a manifold, a superficial connection on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
has been
constructed from the connection on
\mathcal{G}
in
[
Wal16
, Prop. 3.3.1]
; note that in our later constructions, we will always work with bundle gerbes over manifolds.
The bundle gerbe multiplication
induces, for all triples of paths
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
with
sitting instants and the same start and end points, a
fusion product

\displaystyle\lambda\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\gamma_{2}}\star\gamma_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\gamma_{3}}\star\gamma_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\gamma_{3}}\star\gamma_{1}}\ ,

(2.30)

where
\star
denotes the concatenation of paths and
\overline{\gamma}
is
the path
t\longmapsto\gamma(1-t)
.
The fusion product depends smoothly on the paths, is parallel with respect to
the superficial connection, and is associative. The connection and fusion product
satisfy one further compatibility condition, related to the rotation of all loops
involved by
180^{\circ}
(see
[
Wal16
, Definition 2.1.5]
).
A line bundle over
LX
admitting all the structures discussed above
is a
fusion line bundle with superficial connection
.

For
X=M
a manifold, transgression extends to a functor
\mathcal{T}
from
\mathtt{h}\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
, the 1-category
obtained from
\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
by identifying isomorphic 1-morphisms, to
the category of fusion line bundles with superficial connection over
LM
.
The central result of
[
Wal16
]
is that
\mathcal{T}
defines
an equivalence of categories. An explicit inverse
functor
\mathcal{R}
is constructed
in
[
Wal16
]
and is
called
regression
.

3
Group extensions from principal bundles

In this section we construct group extensions from group actions on manifolds with principal bundles.
We generalise this extension to
higher geometry in Section
5
.
We present two perspectives on this group extension. The first one is
global. The second one is local
and can be formulated in terms of the parallel transport of an
auxiliary connection on a principal bundle.

3.1
Global description

Let
H
be a Lie group and
P\longrightarrow M
a principal
H
-bundle on a
manifold
M
; principal
H
-bundles on
M
and isomorphisms form a
groupoid which we denote by
{\mathrm{Bun}}_{H}(M)
.
We consider a Lie group action

\displaystyle\Phiit\colon G\times M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M

(3.1)

\displaystyle(g,x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Phiit_{g}(x)=\Phiit(g,x)

(3.2)

on the base manifold
M
, and ask whether and how this action lifts to
P
.
An action of a Lie group
G
on
M
can equivalently be written as a smooth homomorphism of groups
\Phiit\colon G\longrightarrow\mathrm{Diff}(M)
, where
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
is the diffeological group of
diffeomorphisms
M\longrightarrow M
. In general, the action of
G
does not lift to
P
.
Instead, we will construct a group extension

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow 1

(3.3)

of
G
by the gauge group
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
of
P
. The group
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
acts on the total
space
P
in a way compatible with the action of
G
on
M
.
We show that it is the universal extension of
G
having this
property.

Remark 3.4
.

The extension can be constructed as the pullback of
the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\longrightarrow\mathsf{Aut}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Diff}_{P}(M)\longrightarrow 1

(3.5)

of diffeological groups along
\Phiit
, where
\mathsf{Aut}_{G}(P)
is the group of
G
-equivariant diffeomorphisms
of
P
and
\mathrm{Diff}_{P}(M)
is the subgroup of diffeomorphisms of
M
which admit an equivariant lift to
P
. In the following we
present a different construction which generalises directly to bundle gerbes.

We can pull back the bundle
P
along the source and target maps of
the action groupoid

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{M}}

(3.6)

We define a bundle

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}G\qquad\mbox{with}\quad{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)_{|g}\coloneqq{\mathrm{Bun}}_{H}(M)(P,\Phiit_{g}^{*}P)

(3.7)

for all
g\in G
,
where
{\mathrm{Bun}}_{H}(M)(P,\Phiit_{g}^{*}P)
is the collection of gauge transformations from
P
to
\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
.
In order for
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
to be a bundle over
G
, we must ensure that
the fibres of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
are actually pairwise
diffeomorphic.
It might happen that a pullback bundle
\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
is no longer isomorphic to
P
and hence the fibre over
g

is empty. As an example,
consider the action of the group
G=\mathbb{Z}
on the 2-torus
M={\mathbb{T}}^{2}

generated by an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism
f
, and let
P\longrightarrow{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
be a
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
-bundle with non-trivial Chern class.
Then
[f^{*}P]=-[P]
, and thus
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(P)_{|1}={\mathrm{Bun}}_{{\operatorname{U}}(1)}(P,f^{*}P)=\varnothing
.
Hence in (
3.7
) we have to ensure that the fibres of

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
are actually all non-trivial.

We restrict our attention to
connected
Lie groups
G
; otherwise,
if
G
is not connected,
we consider only the connected component of the identity
e\in G
. We show that in this case
the fibres are always non-trivial:
we need to show that for any
g\in G
the fibre of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
over
g
is non-empty.
That is, we need to show that there exists an isomorphism
P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
of
H
-bundles over
M
.
Let
f_{P}\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathsf{B}}H
be a map that classifies the bundle
P\longrightarrow M
.
Then
\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
is classified by the map
f_{P}\circ\Phiit_{g}\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathsf{B}}H
.
Since
G
is connected, we can find a smooth path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow G
with
\gamma(0)=e
and
\gamma(1)=g
.
Consider the smooth map

\displaystyle\Phiit_{\gamma}\colon[0,1]\times M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M

(3.8)

\displaystyle(t,x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Phiit_{\gamma(t)}(x)\ .

(3.9)

We can postcompose this map by
f_{P}
to obtain a homotopy

f_{P}\circ\Phiit_{\gamma}\colon[0,1]\times M\longrightarrow{\mathsf{B}}H

(3.10)

from
f_{P}
to
f_{P}\circ\Phiit_{g}
.
This shows that there exists a bundle isomorphism
P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
.
We note for later use that this argument generalises to
n
-gerbes
\mathcal{G}
, as these are classified by maps
f_{\mathcal{G}}\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathsf{B}}^{n+1}{\operatorname{U}}(1)
.

In order to equip the set
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
with a diffeology, we note that

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
can be identified with the subspace of the Cartesian product of the
space of
H
-equivariant diffeomorphisms

P\longrightarrow P
which cover the action of an arbitrary element
g\in G
on
M

with
G
,
and equip
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
with the subspace diffeology. Concretely, for
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, a map
f\colon c\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
is a plot if and only if the composition
\pi\circ f\colon c\longrightarrow G
is smooth
and the induced map
\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{f}^{*}P
is an isomorphism in
{\mathrm{Bun}}_{H}(c\times M)
, where

\operatorname{pr}_{M}\colon c\times M\longrightarrow M
is the projection onto
M

and
\Phiit_{f}=\Phiit\circ(f\times 1_{M})
.
The automorphism group or group of gauge transformations

{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\coloneqq{\mathrm{Bun}}_{H}(M)(P,P)

(3.11)

acts simply and transitively on each fibre
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)_{|g}
from the right via precomposition.
The set
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
forms a diffeological group with respect to the composition of
automorphisms and the smooth structure induced from the mapping space diffeology on
P^{P}
.

Proposition 3.12
.

\pi\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
is a principal
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
-bundle on
G
.

Proof.

We verify
that the map
\pi\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
is a subduction. Let
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
be
a plot.
We can pick an isomorphism
\varphi_{f}\colon\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{f}^{*}P
(since
c
is contractible) and define the map

\displaystyle\widehat{f}\colon c

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)

(3.13)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\varphi_{f|\{x\}\times M}\ .

(3.14)

The map
\widehat{f}
is a smooth lift of the plot
f
, showing that
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G

is a subduction.

The map

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\times_{G}{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\times{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)

(3.15)

\displaystyle(\varphi\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P,\varphi^{\prime}\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\varphi,\varphi^{-1}\circ\varphi^{\prime})

(3.16)

provides a smooth inverse to the map
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\times{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\times_{G}{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
from (
2.15
), and the result follows.
∎

Proposition 3.17
.

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
is a diffeological group. The principal bundle
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
is part of an extension of diffeological groups

1\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow 1\ .

(3.18)

Proof.

To complete the proof we need to equip
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
with a
diffeological group structure such that the map
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G

becomes a morphism of diffeological groups.
Consider isomorphisms
\psi\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
and
\phi\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g^{\prime}}^{*}P
for
g,g^{\prime}\in G
.
We set

\mu(\psi,\phi)\coloneqq\Phiit_{g^{\prime}}^{*}\psi\circ\phi\ \colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g^{\prime}}^{*}P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g^{\prime}}^{*}\Phiit_{g}^{*}P=\Phiit_{g\,g^{\prime}}^{*}P\ .

(3.19)

This is associative by the associativity of pullbacks, the multiplication in
G
, and composition of morphisms. The inverse
of an element
\psi\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
with respect to
\mu
is the isomorphism

P=\Phiit_{g^{-1}}^{*}\Phiit_{g}^{*}P\xrightarrow{\Phiit_{g^{-1}}^{*}\psi^{-1}}\Phiit_{g^{-1}}^{*}P\ ,

and the result follows from the observation that these maps are
smooth.
∎

Proposition 3.20
.

The group
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
acts smoothly on
P
, lifting the action of
G
on
M
.
It is universal in the following sense:
let
\widehat{G}
be a Lie group,
\varphi\colon\widehat{G}\longrightarrow G

a Lie group homomorphism and
\widehat{\psi}\colon\widehat{G}\times P\longrightarrow P

an action of
\widehat{G}
on
P
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}\times P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\varpi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit}

(3.21)

commute, where
\varpi\colon P\longrightarrow M
is the bundle projection. Then there exists a unique smooth group homomorphism
\widehat{G}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}\times P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\times P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

(3.22)

commutes.

Proof.

The action is via the evaluation

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phiit}\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\times P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P

(3.23)

\displaystyle(\phi,p)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi(p)=\phi_{|\varpi(p)}(p)\ .

(3.24)

The unique smooth group homomorphism in the universality statement is

\displaystyle\widehat{G}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)

(3.25)

\displaystyle\widehat{g}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big(\widehat{\psi}_{\widehat{g}}\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{\varphi(\widehat{g})}^{*}P\big)\ ,

(3.26)

and the result follows.
∎

The construction of the group
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
is functorial in
P
, i.e.
an
isomorphism of bundles
\psi\colon P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}

induces an isomorphism of group extensions

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P^{\prime})

(3.27)

\displaystyle(f\colon P\longrightarrow g^{*}P)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Big(P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\psi^{-1}}P\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}g^{*}P\xrightarrow{g^{*}\psi}g^{*}P^{\prime}\Big)\ .

(3.28)

3.2
Equivariant bundles

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
M
a manifold with
G
-action
\Phiit\colon G\times M\longrightarrow M
, and

P
a principal
H
-bundle over
M
. A
G
-equivariant structure
on

P
consists of an isomorphism
\chi\colon\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}P\longrightarrow\Phiit^{*}P
of principal bundles over
G\times M
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\Phiit_{g\,g^{\prime}}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\Phiit_{g}(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{(g\,g^{\prime},x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{(g,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{g}^{*}\chi_{(g^{\prime},x)}}

(3.29)

commutes for all
g,g^{\prime}\in G
and
x\in M
. We denote by
\mathcal{E}(P)

the set of equivariant structures on
P
.
A
splitting

s
of
\pi\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
is a smooth
group homomorphism
s\colon G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
such that

\pi\circ s=1_{G}
. We denote the set of splittings of
\pi\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
by
\mathcal{S}(G;{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P))
.

Proposition 3.30
.

There is a natural bijection of sets
\Xi\colon\mathcal{E}(P)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(G;{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P))
. In particular, the bundle
P
admits
an equivariant structure if and only if the extension

1\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow 1

is trivial as an extension of diffeological groups.

Proof.

Let
(P,\chi)
be an equivariant bundle. We define

\Xi(P,\chi)(g)\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
to be
\chi_{|\{g\}\times M}
. The inverse
\Xi^{-1}\colon\mathcal{S}(G;{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P))\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}(P)
can be constructed by
sending a splitting
s\colon G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
to the
isomorphism
\Xi^{-1}(s)\colon\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}P\longrightarrow\Phiit^{*}P

which is given by

s(g)(x)\colon P_{x}\longrightarrow P_{\Phiit_{g}(x)}
at
(g,x)\in G\times M
.
∎

Let
(P,\chi)
and
(P^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
be
G
-equivariant
H
-bundles on
M
. An isomorphism
\psi\colon P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}
is
equivariant
if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{g}^{*}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{g}^{*}P^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{g}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{g}^{*}\psi}

(3.31)

commutes for all
g\in G
.
The equivariant structures on
P
and
P^{\prime}
can be described by smooth group homomorphisms

s_{P}\colon G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
and

s_{P^{\prime}}\colon G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P^{\prime})
.
Since the isomorphism
\widehat{\psi}
defined in
(
3.27
)
intertwines the action of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
and
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P^{\prime})
on
P

and
P^{\prime}
, respectively, it follows that
\psi
is equivariant if and
only if
s_{P^{\prime}}=\widehat{\psi}\circ s_{P}
. Hence the smooth group
extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}
contains all information
on equivariance.

3.3
Description via parallel transport

The extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
can be described more explicitly using the parallel transport of a
connection on
P
, as we will now explain.
In Section
6
we apply this to the
description of magnetic translations in quantum mechanics.
We consider a principal
H
-bundle
P\longrightarrow M
.
Let
P_{0}G
denote the diffeological space of smooth paths in
G
with sitting instants based at
e\in G
,
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}\colon P_{0}G\longrightarrow G
the evaluation at the end point,
(P_{0}G)^{[2]}
the fibre product
P_{0}G\times_{G}P_{0}G
with respect
to
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}
, and
LM
the space
of smooth loops in
M
.
We denote by
\star
the concatenation of paths.
For a path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow G
we denote by
\overline{\gamma}
the precomposition
of
\gamma
with

\displaystyle[0,1]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[0,1]

(3.32)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1-t\ .

(3.33)

For a path
\gamma\in P_{0}G
and a point
x\in M
, set

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}\colon[0,1]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M

(3.34)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Phiit_{\gamma(t)}(x)\ .

(3.35)

Endow
P
with an arbitrary connection
A
.
The
H
-bundle
P
with connection then induces a principal
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
-bundle on
G
as follows:
we set

\mathcal{L}_{G}\coloneqq\big(P_{0}G\times{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\big)\big/{\sim}\ ,

(3.36)

where we define the equivalence relation

(\gamma,\phi)\sim\big(\alpha,\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha,\gamma)\circ\phi\big)\qquad\text{with}\quad\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha,\gamma)(x)\coloneqq\operatorname{hol}\big(P,(\overline{\alpha}\star\gamma)_{x}\big)\ \in\ \mathsf{End}(P_{x})

(3.37)

for all
(\gamma,\alpha)\in(P_{0}G)^{[2]}
and
x\in M
,
and we interpret the holonomy of
P
along a loop starting and ending
at
x
as an endomorphism of the fibre
P_{x}
.
Note that, with this notation, we have defined a smooth map
\operatorname{hol}(P,-)\colon(P_{0}G)^{[2]}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
.
We endow
\mathcal{L}_{G}
with
the quotient diffeology.

Then the
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
-bundle
\mathcal{L}_{G}\longrightarrow G
can be defined in terms of descent data
as follows:
the action
\Phiit
of
G
on
M
induces a smooth map

\displaystyle L\Phiit\colon(P_{0}G)^{[2]}\times M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow LM

(3.38)

\displaystyle(\gamma,\alpha,x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\overline{\alpha}\star\gamma)_{x}\ .

(3.39)

Explicitly,

(\overline{\alpha}\star\gamma)_{x}(t)=\Phiit_{(\overline{\alpha}\star\gamma)(t)}(x)\ \in\ M

(3.40)

for all
t\in[0,1]
and
x\in M
.
The descent data for the bundle
\mathcal{L}_{G}
consists of the subduction

P_{0}G\longrightarrow G
, the trivial bundle
P_{0}G\times{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)\longrightarrow P_{0}G
, and the smooth map

\displaystyle{\mathsf{g}}\colon(P_{0}G)^{[2]}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)

(3.41)

\displaystyle(\gamma,\alpha)

\displaystyle\longmapsto{\mathsf{g}}(\gamma,\alpha)=\operatorname{hol}(P,\overline{\alpha}\star\gamma)\ .

(3.42)

Proposition 3.43
.

The total space
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is a smooth group extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\ .}

(3.44)

Proof.

Let
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
be two paths in
G
.
The evaluation
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}\colon P_{0}G\longrightarrow G
is a group homomorphism with
respect to the pointwise product of paths.

Let
x\in M
be an arbitrary point.
To any triple
(x,\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
, we can associate a map

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}(x,\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\colon|\Delta^{2}|

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M

(3.45)

\displaystyle(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Phiit_{\gamma(t_{1})}\big(\gamma^{\prime}_{x}(t_{2})\big)\ ,

(3.46)

where
|\Delta^{2}|
is the standard topological 2-simplex with
|\Delta^{2}|\cong\{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,0\leq t_{2}\leq t_{1}\leq 1\}
.
Diagrammatically, this is a homotopy

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(1)\cdot\gamma^{\prime}(1)\cdot x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}(1)\cdot x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}(t)\cdot x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}(t)\cdot\gamma(t)\cdot x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(t)\cdot\gamma^{\prime}(1)\cdot x}

(3.47)

between the product path
\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime}\in P_{0}G
and the concatenated path
(\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime}(1))\star\gamma^{\prime}\in P_{0}G
.

For
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in P_{0}G
and
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
, we define

\mu\big((\gamma,\phi),(\gamma^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\big)\coloneqq\big(\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime},\,{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma\,\left(\gamma^{\prime}(1)\right)}\circ(\Phiit_{\gamma^{\prime}(1)}^{*}\phi)\,\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\phi^{\prime}\big)\,,

(3.48)

where we denote by
{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}
the isomorphism
P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{\gamma(1)}^{*}P

defined at a point
x\in M
by the parallel transport along the path
\gamma_{x}
.
This is well-defined: let
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in P_{0}G
with
\gamma(1)=\alpha(1)
and
\gamma^{\prime}(1)=\alpha^{\prime}(1)
.
Then

\displaystyle\mu\big((\alpha,\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha,\gamma)\circ\phi)

\displaystyle,(\alpha^{\prime},\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\circ\phi^{\prime})\big)

(3.49)

\displaystyle=\big(\alpha\,\alpha^{\prime},\,{\mathtt{pt}}_{\alpha\,\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\Phiit_{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}^{*}({\mathtt{pt}}_{\alpha}\circ\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha,\gamma)\circ\phi^{\prime})\,\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\circ\phi^{\prime}\big)

(3.50)

\displaystyle=\big(\alpha\,\alpha^{\prime},\,\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha\,\alpha^{\prime},\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime})\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\Phiit_{\gamma^{\prime}(1)}^{*}({\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}\circ\phi^{\prime})\,\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\phi^{\prime}\big)

(3.51)

\displaystyle=\big(\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime},\,{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\Phiit_{\gamma^{\prime}(1)}^{*}({\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}\circ\phi^{\prime})\,\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\phi^{\prime}\big)

(3.52)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((\gamma,\phi),(\gamma^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\big)\ ,

(3.53)

where we used
\Phiit_{\gamma^{\prime}(1)}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}={\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma\,(\gamma^{\prime}(1))}
.
Associativity then follows immediately from the associativity of the
products in
P_{0}G
and
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
, together with associativity of
taking pullbacks. Smoothness follows from the definition of the
quotient diffeology and the smooth dependence of parallel transport on
the path.
∎

Remark 3.54
.

For abelian structure group
H
, we can use the fact that parallel
transport commutes with gauge transformations to get the simplified expression

\displaystyle\mu\big((\gamma,\phi),(\gamma^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\big)=\big(\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime},\,\operatorname{hol}(P,\partial|\Delta^{2}|)\,(\Phiit_{\gamma^{\prime}(1)}^{*}\phi)\circ\phi^{\prime}\big)

(3.55)

for the multiplication (
3.48
).

\vartriangleleft

Remark 3.56
.

If
G
is abelian, then the multiplicative structure yields isomorphisms

\mathcal{L}_{G|g}\times\mathcal{L}_{G|g^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{G|g\,g^{\prime}}=\mathcal{L}_{G|g^{\prime}\,g}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{G|g^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{L}_{G|g}

(3.57)

for all
g,g^{\prime}\in G
.
That is, the group extension
\mathcal{L}_{G}
spoils the commutativity of
G
, since its fibres multiply commutatively only up to coherent isomorphism.

\vartriangleleft

We summarise the connection to the construction from Section
3.1
in

Proposition 3.58
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group, and let
P\longrightarrow M
be
a principal
H
-bundle on a manifold
M
with smooth
G
-action.
The map

\displaystyle\Gamma\colon\mathcal{L}_{G}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)

(3.59)

\displaystyle\big[(\gamma,\phi)\big]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big({\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}\circ\phi\colon P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{\gamma(1)}^{*}P\big)

(3.60)

is an isomorphism of diffeological group extensions of
G
.

Proof.

The map is well-defined: consider two representatives

(\gamma,\phi)
and
(\alpha,\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha,\gamma)\circ\phi)
of the same equivalence class in
\mathcal{L}_{G}
, and calculate

{\mathtt{pt}}_{\alpha}\circ\operatorname{hol}(P,\alpha,\gamma)\circ\phi={\mathtt{pt}}_{\alpha}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\overline{\alpha}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}\circ\phi={\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}\circ\phi\ .

The map is bijective, because two gauge transformations

P\longrightarrow\Phiit_{g}^{*}P
differ by exactly one gauge transformation of
P
.
It also follows directly from the definition that
\Gamma
is a morphism of extensions.
We check that
\Gamma
is a group homomorphism: for
[(\gamma,\phi)],\,[(\gamma^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})]\in\mathcal{L}_{G}
we compute

\displaystyle\mu\big(\Gamma(\gamma,\phi),\Gamma(\gamma^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu({\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}\circ\phi,\,{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\phi^{\prime})

(3.61)

\displaystyle=\Phiit_{\gamma^{\prime}(1)}^{*}({\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma}\circ\phi)\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\phi^{\prime}

(3.62)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\big(\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime},\,{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma\,\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma\,(\gamma^{\prime}(1))}\circ(\Phiit_{\gamma^{\prime}(1)}^{*}\phi)\,\circ{\mathtt{pt}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\phi^{\prime}\big)

(3.63)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\Big(\mu\big((\gamma,\phi),(\gamma^{\prime},\phi)\big)\Big)\ .

(3.64)

Finally, we verify that
\Gamma
is smooth. Let
f\colon c\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{G}

be a plot admitting a lift
\widehat{f}\colon c\longrightarrow P_{0}G\times{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
. We denote the components of
\widehat{f}
by
\widehat{f}_{\gamma}

and
\widehat{f}_{{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)}
. It is enough to show that

\displaystyle\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Phiit_{\widehat{f}_{\gamma}}^{*}P

\displaystyle P_{x}\ni p

\displaystyle\longmapsto{\mathtt{pt}}_{\widehat{f}_{\gamma}(u)}\big(\widehat{f}_{{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)}(u)(p)\big)\in P_{\Phiit_{\widehat{f}_{\gamma}(u)(1)}(x)}

is a gauge transformation. This follows from the smoothness of parallel transport
(recalled in Section
4.1
below).
∎

Corollary 3.65
.

The action
\Phiit\colon G\times M\longrightarrow M
lifts to an action

\widehat{\Phiit}\colon\mathcal{L}_{G}\times P\longrightarrow P\,

(3.66)

which covers the action of
G
on
M
.

4
A global approach to parallel transport for bundle gerbes

In Section
3
we have constructed two diffeological
groups,
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
and
\mathcal{L}_{G}
, which extend
G
and control the
existence of
G
-equivariant structures on a principal bundle
P
over

M
.
The key to constructing
\mathcal{L}_{G}
, as well as to comparing the groups
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
and
\mathcal{L}_{G}
(see Section
3.3
), was the parallel transport on the principal bundle
P
.

If one replaces the principal bundle
P
by a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
on
M
, there exist categorified versions of both these constructions which will be given in Section
5
.
However, in order to write down the categorification of
\mathcal{L}_{G}
we need a notion of parallel transport for
\mathcal{G}
.
In this section we give a definition of parallel transport for
\mathcal{G}
suited for our purposes and explicitly construct such a parallel transport from any connection on
\mathcal{G}
.
Our construction relies heavily on Waldorf’s transgression-regression machine
[
Wal16
]
.

There is a different approach to the parallel transport on a bundle gerbe developed by Schreiber and Waldorf
[
SW09
,
SW11
,
SW17
]
.
It relies on their technology of transport functors and is based on local constructions, which are then glued to global objects.
In
[
Wal18
]
, this has been extended to a canonical assignment of a parallel transport (in terms of a transport 2-functor) to any principal 2-bundle with connection whose structure group is a Lie 2-group.

Here, in contrast, we directly define and construct a global version of parallel transport suitable for our purposes.
As our main goal in this paper is the construction of categorified smooth group extensions, we leave it for future work to prove in detail that our notion of parallel transport for
\mathcal{G}
agrees with that of Schreiber and Waldorf, and instead focus on building the necessary input for the constructions in Section
5
.

4.1
A path space approach to parallel transport on line bundles

Before we give our definition and construction of the parallel transport for bundle gerbes, we recast the parallel transport on line bundles from a global perspective.
Our notion of parallel transport for bundle gerbes will then be a categorification of this picture.
Let
M
be a connected smooth manifold, and fix a base point
x\in M
;
if
M
is not connected, we restrict to its connected components individually.
We denote by
PM
the diffeological space of smooth paths with sitting instants in
M

and by
P_{0}M
the subspace of paths starting at
x
.
Let
L
be a line bundle on
M
with connection. The smoothness of the parallel
transport on
L
can be encoded as follows: for
t\in[0,1]
, denote by
{\mathrm{ev}}_{t}\colon PM\longrightarrow M
,
\gamma\longmapsto\gamma(t)
, the evaluation at
t
.
Parallel transport on
L
is in particular an isomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathtt{pt}}^{L}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}L

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}L

\displaystyle L_{\gamma(0)}={\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}L_{\gamma}\ni\ell

\displaystyle\longmapsto{\mathtt{pt}}^{L}_{\gamma}(\ell)\in L_{\gamma(1)}={\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}L_{\gamma}

of line bundles over
PM
.

There is a different way to construct this isomorphism using descent.
Via transgression and regression

[
Wal12b
]
we can construct a bundle
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)
, which is isomorphic to
L
, from the descent data

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{U}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{0}{M})^{[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P}_{0}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}

(4.1)

with respect to the path fibration.
Here
f
is constructed as in Section
3.3
from the holonomy of
L
.
The total space of the line bundle
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)
consists of
equivalence classes of pairs
(\gamma,\zeta)\in P_{0}M\times\mathbb{C}
, where the equivalence
relation reads as
(\gamma_{1},\zeta)\sim(\gamma_{2},f(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\,\zeta)
for

(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in(P_{0}{M})^{[2]}
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
.
An isomorphism
g_{\chi}\colon\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)\longrightarrow L

can be constructed by picking a
trivialisation
\chi\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow L_{x}
of the fibre of
L

over the base point
x\in M
and defining

g_{\chi}\big([\gamma,\zeta]\big)\coloneqq{\mathtt{pt}}^{L}_{\gamma}\big(\chi(\zeta)\big)\ .

The pullbacks
{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)
and
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)
are thus described in terms of descent data with respect to the covers

{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M\cong P_{0}M\times_{M}PM\longrightarrow M
and
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M\cong PM\times_{M}P_{0}M\longrightarrow M
, respectively.
In order to construct the isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)}
explicitly we use the space (see Figure
1
)

P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\coloneqq{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M\times_{PM}{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M\cong P_{0}M\times_{M}PM\times_{M}P_{0}M\ ,

(4.2)

which fits into the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PM}

(4.3)

\begin{overpic}[Sketch_Path_space.pdf]
\put(90.0,225.0){$P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M$}
\put(0.0,145.0){${\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M$}
\put(165.0,145.0){${\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M$}
\put(90.0,0.0){$PM$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 1:
Elements in the spaces
P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M
,
{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M
and
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M
.

An isomorphism from
{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)
to
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)
can be described by
a function
P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)
which is compatible with the descent data. There is a
canonical choice for such a function given by

\displaystyle P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\longrightarrow LM\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{hol}\ }{\operatorname{U}}(1)\ .

(4.4)

Concretely, the induced map is

\displaystyle{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)

\displaystyle[\gamma_{xy},\zeta]

\displaystyle\longmapsto{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)}_{(\gamma_{xy},\gamma_{yz},\gamma_{xz})}[\gamma_{xy},\zeta]=\big[\gamma_{xz},\operatorname{hol}\big(L,\overline{\gamma_{xz}}\star(\gamma_{yz}\star\gamma_{xy})\big)\,\zeta\big]\ ,

where
x
is the fixed base point of
M
while
y,z\in M
are arbitrary points, and
\gamma_{ab}
denotes a path from
a
to
b
for
a,b\in\{x,y,z\}
.
The holonomy appearing
here agrees with
\operatorname{hol}(L,(\overline{\gamma_{xz}}\star\gamma_{yz})\star\gamma_{xy})
. The construction is independent
of all choices involved.
Now a straightforward computation shows that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}g_{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}g_{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(L)}}

(4.5)

commutes. This shows that we can construct the parallel transport on
L
completely in terms
of the descent data with respect to the path fibration. For bundle gerbes, the analogue of

{\mathtt{pt}}^{L}
is difficult to define directly, but an analogous approach via descent data on
P_{0}M
allows us to solve this problem.

4.2
Global definition of parallel transport on bundle gerbes

As before, let
M
be a manifold, and let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}(M)
be a bundle gerbe on
M
.
A parallel transport on
\mathcal{G}
should in particular be a 1-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}

(4.6)

in
\mathrm{BGrb}(PM)
with a 2-isomorphism

{\mathsf{c}}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\cong 1_{\mathcal{G}}\ ,

(4.7)

where
{\mathsf{c}}\colon M\longrightarrow PM
is the embedding of
M
into
PM
as constant paths.
Note that the parallel transport is, in general, an isomorphism of
gerbes
without
connections. The same is true for bundles: the parallel transport on a vector
bundle with connection respects the connection if and only if the connection is flat.

To proceed further, we need some definitions. Let
i,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leq i\leq n
.
For each
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1})\in[0,1]^{n-1}
, define a smooth map

\displaystyle\iota^{n}_{i;s}\colon[0,1]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[0,1]^{n}

(4.8)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\longmapsto(s_{1},\ldots,s_{i-1},t,s_{i},\ldots,s_{n-1})\ .

(4.9)

Consider the diffeological spaces
P^{n}M
which are defined by the sets of
all maps

\Sigmait\colon[0,1]^{n}\longrightarrow M

satisfying the following property: for all
i=1,\ldots,n
, there exists
\epsilon_{i}>0
such the map
\Sigmait\circ\iota^{n}_{i;s}:[0,1]\longrightarrow M
is locally constant on
[0,\epsilon_{i})\sqcup(1-\epsilon_{i},1]
.
Note that in a plot of
P^{n}M
, the
\epsilon_{i}
do not have to be
constant over the domain of the plot. The space
P^{n}M
describes
n
-cubes in
M
with sitting instants in all directions perpendicular to the faces of
[0,1]^{n}
; that is,
P^{n}M
describes iterated smooth homotopies of paths with sitting instants in
M
.

We also consider the subspaces
P^{n}_{*}M
of the diffeological spaces
P^{n}M

consisting of maps
\Sigmait\in P^{n}M
satisfying the following property: for
all
s\in[0,1]^{n-1}
, and for each
j=1,\ldots,n-1
such that

s_{j}\in\{0,1\}
, the map
\Sigmait\circ\iota^{n}_{i;s}
is constant
for all
i>j
. The space
P^{n}_{*}M
describes iterated smooth homotopies with fixed endpoints in
M
.
For example,
P_{*}M=PM
is the space of paths with sitting instants,
P^{2}_{*}M
consists of maps
\Sigmait\in P^{2}M
such that

\Sigmait(0,t)=\Sigmait(0,0)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Sigmait(1,t)=\Sigmait(1,0)

for all
t\in[0,1]
and so is the space of homotopies
of paths with fixed endpoints in
M
, and an element in
P^{3}_{*}M
is a
family of fixed-ends homotopies between two fixed paths in
M
.
We say that an element
\Sigmait
in
P^{n}_{*}M
or in
P^{n}M
is

thin
if its differential
\Sigmait_{*}
has non-maximal ranks
\mathrm{rk}(\Sigmait_{*|s})<n
for all
s\in[0,1]^{n}
.

Let
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in[0,1]^{k}
and
n=k+l
.
For
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\leq n
, we define a map

\iota^{n}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l};s}\colon[0,1]^{l}\longrightarrow[0,1]^{n}

(4.10)

which inserts the coordinates of
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l})\in[0,1]^{l}
into the
k
-tuple
s
such that

\big(\iota^{n}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l};s}(t)\big)_{j}=t_{j}

(4.11)

for every
j\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}
.
The maps
\iota^{n}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l};s}\colon[0,1]^{l}\longrightarrow[0,1]^{n}
induce maps

\iota^{n*}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l};s}\colon P^{n}M\longrightarrow P^{l}M

(4.12)

which map
P_{*}^{n}M
to
P^{l}_{*}M
.

For the parallel transport of a bundle gerbe, there should also be a 2-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}\colon(\iota^{2*}_{1;0})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\longrightarrow(\iota^{2*}_{1;1})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}

(4.13)

in
\mathrm{BGrb}(P^{2}_{*}M)
.
In other words, any map
\Sigmait\in P^{2}_{*}M
is in particular a smooth map
[0,1]^{2}\longrightarrow M
from the square to
M
.
This map is constant on the vertical edges of the square.
Pulling back the isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
to the horizontal edges of the square gives two 1-morphisms
\mathcal{G}_{\Sigmait(0,0)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\Sigmait(1,0)}
, and the 2-morphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
relates these.
The data
({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
are required to satisfy the following two properties, which are motivated by
[
BW19
,
BW18
,
Wal16
]
:

(1)

For any two thin maps
\Sigmait,\Sigmait^{\prime}\in P^{2}_{*}M
with
\Sigmait\circ\iota^{2}_{1;s}=\Sigmait^{\prime}\circ\iota^{2}_{1;s}
for
s=0,1
,
there is an equality

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait}={\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait^{\prime}}\ .

(4.14)

That is, the 2-morphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
evaluated on any pair of fixed-ends thin homotopies between any two given paths in
M
gives the same result.

(2)

We further demand that for any thin map
h\in P^{3}_{*}M
, there is
an equality

(\iota^{3*}_{1,2;0})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}{}_{|h}=(\iota^{3*}_{1,2;1})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}{}_{|h}\ .

(4.15)

As we will be using
P^{n}_{*}M
mostly for
n=0,1,2
, we adopt the convention to write
\gamma_{2}\star\gamma_{1}
for the concatenation of smooth paths in
M
, and if
\Sigmait,\Sigmait^{\prime}\in P_{*}^{2}M
are homotopies
\Sigmait\colon\gamma\longrightarrow\gamma^{\prime}
and
\Sigmait^{\prime}\colon\gamma^{\prime}\longrightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}
, we write
\Sigmait^{\prime}\star_{2}\Sigmait\colon\gamma\longrightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}
for their vertical concatenation.
If
\Xi\colon\alpha\longrightarrow\alpha^{\prime}
is a further homotopy in
P_{*}^{2}M
such that the starting point of
\alpha
is the endpoint of
\gamma
, then we write
\Xi\star\Sigmait\colon\alpha\star\gamma\longrightarrow\alpha^{\prime}\star\gamma^{\prime}
for the horizontal concatenation of the homotopies.
We will also often use the term ‘composition’ instead of ‘concatenation’.

Definition 4.16
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold.
A
parallel transport
on a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}(M)
is a quadruple
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}=({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star},\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}})
of

(1)

a 1-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}

(4.17)

of bundle gerbes over
PM
,

(2)

a 2-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}\colon(\iota^{2*}_{1;0})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\longrightarrow(\iota^{2*}_{1;1})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}

(4.18)

in
\mathrm{BGrb}(P^{2}_{*}M)
,

(3)

a 2-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star}\colon\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\longrightarrow(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,\star\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}

(4.19)

over
PM\times_{M}PM
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{1}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
are the respective
projections of
PM\times_{M}PM
to the first and second factors, and

(4)

a 2-isomorphism

\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}}\colon{\mathsf{c}}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\longrightarrow 1_{\mathcal{G}}

(4.20)

over
M
, where
{\mathsf{c}}\colon M\longrightarrow PM
is the inclusion of
M
as the space of constant paths.

These data are required to satisfy properties (
4.14
) and (
4.15
).
Due to property (
4.14
), there is a
canonical
2-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|(\gamma_{3}\star\gamma_{2})\star\gamma_{1}}\cong{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{3}\star(\gamma_{2}\star\gamma_{1})}

(4.21)

for every
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\in PM\times_{M}PM\times_{M}PM
, and we demand that
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star}
is coherently associative with respect to this isomorphism.
The morphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star}
also needs to be compatible with the
unitors in
\mathrm{BGrb}(PM)
and sit in a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{2}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{2}\star\gamma_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\alpha_{2}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\alpha_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\alpha_{2}\star\alpha_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star|\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait_{2}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait_{2}\star\Sigmait_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star|\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}}}

(4.22)

for all
x,y,z\in M
, all paths
\gamma_{1},\alpha_{1}
from
x
to
y
, all paths
\gamma_{2},\alpha_{2}
from
y
to
z
in
M
, and for all fixed-ends homotopies
\Sigmait_{i}\colon\gamma_{i}\longrightarrow\alpha_{i}
.
Furthermore,
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
has to respect vertical composition and satisfy the interchange law

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait^{\prime}_{1}\star\Sigmait^{\prime}_{0}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait^{\prime}_{1}\star\Sigmait^{\prime}_{0}}={\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait^{\prime}_{1}\star_{2}\Sigmait_{1}}\circ_{2}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2|\Sigmait^{\prime}_{0}\star_{2}\Sigmait_{0}}

(4.23)

for all points
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}\in M
, all paths
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}
from
x_{i}
to
x_{i+1}
, and for all fixed-ends homotopies
\Sigmait_{i}\colon\alpha_{i}\longrightarrow\beta_{i}
and
\Sigmait^{\prime}_{i}\colon\beta_{i}\longrightarrow\gamma_{i}
with
i=0,1
.

Remark 4.24
.

The associativity condition in detail reads as follows:
for every concatenable triple
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
of
paths in
M
there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{3}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{2}\star\gamma_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{3}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{2}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{3}\star\gamma_{2}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|\gamma_{3}\star(\gamma_{2}\star\gamma_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1|(\gamma_{3}\star\gamma_{2})\star\gamma_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star|\gamma_{3},\gamma_{2}\star\gamma_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star|\gamma_{3},\gamma_{2}}\circ 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star|\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star|\gamma_{3}\star\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}}}

(4.25)

in
\mathrm{BGrb}(PM\times_{M}PM)
, where the bottom arrow is the canonical 2-isomorphism obtained via (
4.14
) from any reparameterisation of
[0,1]
that yields a homotopy
\gamma_{3}\star(\gamma_{2}\star\gamma_{1})\sim(\gamma_{3}\star\gamma_{2})\star\gamma_{1}
.

\vartriangleleft

Remark 4.26
.

By property (
4.15
), our definition factors through the path 2-groupoid of
M
as defined by Schreiber and Waldorf
[
SW11
,
SW17
]
.
Given a manifold
M
, they construct a 2-groupoid internal to diffeological spaces, whose level sets are essentially
M
,
PM
and the quotient
P^{2}_{*}M/{\sim}
of
P_{*}^{2}M
by thin homotopies.
Note that in this quotient they also implement condition (4.1).

\vartriangleleft

In contrast to the case of parallel transport on vector bundles, we can define morphisms between parallel transports on a given bundle gerbe.

Definition 4.27
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}(M)
be a bundle gerbe on
M
.
Let
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}=({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star},\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}})
and
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}{}^{\mathcal{G}}=({\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}_{1}{}^{\mathcal{G}},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}_{2}{}^{\mathcal{G}},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}_{\star}{}^{\mathcal{G}},\varepsilon^{\prime}{}^{\mathcal{G}})
be two choices of parallel transport on
\mathcal{G}
.
A
morphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}{}^{\mathcal{G}}
of parallel transports on
\mathcal{G}
is a 2-isomorphism
\psi\colon{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}_{1}{}^{\mathcal{G}}
in
\mathrm{BGrb}(PM)
that intertwines the 2-isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
with
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}_{2}{}^{\mathcal{G}}
, the 2-isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\star}
with
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\prime}_{\star}{}^{\mathcal{G}}
, and the 2-isomorphism
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}}
with
\varepsilon^{\prime}{}^{\mathcal{G}}
.
This defines a groupoid
{\mathtt{PT}}(\mathcal{G})
of parallel transports on
\mathcal{G}
.

This notion of morphism of parallel transports is not an analogue of a
gauge transformation, since it does not necessarily come from an
