automorphism of the bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
.

4.3
Construction of the parallel transport

We now proceed to show that every bundle gerbe with connection on a manifold
M
has a canonical parallel transport.
Let
M
be a connected manifold, and fix a base point
x\in M
;
otherwise, if
M
is not connected, we treat the connected components
of
M
separately.
By results of Waldorf
[
Wal16
]
, any bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
is isomorphic to a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
that is defined over the diffeological path fibration
P_{0}M\longrightarrow M
.
Given a choice of base point
x\in M
, Waldorf constructs a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G})
as the regression of the transgression line bundle of
\mathcal{G}
, together with a natural 1-isomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
in the homotopy category of
\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
; that is,
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}
is determined only up to 2-isomorphism.
(We remark, however, that the natural 1-isomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}
from
[
Wal16
]
is determined
canonically
once we fix a preimage of the base point
x\in M
under the surjective submersion
\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow M
underlying the bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
.)

Consider the bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G})\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
with connection on
M
, defined with respect to the path fibration
\pi:P_{0}M\longrightarrow M
.
Its line bundle
L
is the pullback of the transgression line bundle
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow LM
along the map

\displaystyle(P_{0}M)^{[2]}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow LM

(4.28)

\displaystyle(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle\longmapsto\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\star\alpha\ .

(4.29)

By a slight abuse of notation, we also denote this pullback by
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow(P_{0}M)^{[2]}
.

Construction of
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}

We would like to construct a 1-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}

(4.30)

in
\mathrm{BGrb}(PM)
.
For
t=0,1
, the bundle gerbe
{\mathrm{ev}}_{t}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is defined over the subduction
{\mathrm{ev}}_{t}^{*}P_{0}M\longrightarrow PM
.
There are canonical isomorphisms of diffeological spaces

{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M\cong P_{0}M\times_{M}PM\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M\cong PM\times_{M}P_{0}M\ .

(4.31)

Recall from Section
4.1
the space

P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\coloneqq{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M\times_{PM}{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M\cong P_{0}M\times_{M}PM\times_{M}P_{0}M\ .

(4.32)

A point in the total space
P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M
is a triple
(\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1})
of a path
\gamma\in PM
and based paths
\alpha_{t}\in P_{0}M
such that
\gamma(t)=\alpha_{t}(1)
for
t=0,1
.
Any 1-morphism
{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is defined over
(possibly a refinement of) the subduction
\xi:P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\longrightarrow PM
.

There is a smooth map, i.e. a morphism of diffeological spaces

\displaystyle{\mathsf{s}}\colon P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow LM

(4.33)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1})

\displaystyle\longmapsto\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})\ .

(4.34)

There is also the smooth map

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathsf{s}}}\colon P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow LM

(4.35)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star\gamma)\star\alpha_{0}\ .

(4.36)

The maps
{\mathsf{s}}
and
\tilde{{\mathsf{s}}}
are smoothly homotopic via precomposition by a homotopy
h
of piecewise smooth homeomorphisms
[0,1]\longrightarrow[0,1]
; these fail to be smooth exactly at those points of the interval where the concatenations happen, but at these points all three paths have sitting instants, so that at each time the homotopy maps to
LM
, as desired.
For each triple of paths
(\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1})
, this results in a thin homotopy in
LM
from
\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})
to
(\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star\gamma)\star\alpha_{0}
.
By the superficiality of the parallel transport
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}}
on the
transgression line
bundle
[
Wal16
, Definition 2.2.1]
(see also the
end of Section
2.2
), we thus obtain a canonical isomorphism

r\colon{\mathsf{s}}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\tilde{{\mathsf{s}}}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}

(4.37)

in
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{\nabla}(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)
.
The fact that this isomorphism preserves connections is a direct consequence of
[
Wal16
, Lemma 2.3.3]
.
Since
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}}
is thin-invariant, it follows that the morphism
r
is defined independently of the choice of homotopy
h
.

We define a morphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as follows:
its underlying line bundle is the line bundle
{\mathsf{s}}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M
.
To turn this into a morphism of bundle gerbes, we need to provide an isomorphism of line bundles

\beta\colon\operatorname{pr}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\otimes\xi_{1}^{*}{\mathsf{s}}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\xi_{0}^{*}{\mathsf{s}}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}

(4.38)

over
(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[2]}
.
Let us unravel this: the fibre product
(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[2]}=P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\times_{PM}P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M
consists of pairs
((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1}))
where
(\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1})
and
(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1})
are elements of
P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M
.
For
t=0,1
, there are the projection maps

\displaystyle\operatorname{pr}_{t}\colon(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[2]}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(P_{0}M)^{[2]}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\big((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\alpha_{t},\alpha^{\prime}_{t})\ .

(4.40)

Thus

\displaystyle(\operatorname{pr}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\otimes\xi_{1}^{*}{\mathsf{s}}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G})_{((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1}))}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha^{\prime}_{0})}\ ,

(4.41)

\displaystyle(\xi_{0}^{*}{\mathsf{s}}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G})_{((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1}))}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star\alpha_{1}}\ .

(4.42)

Let
\lambda\colon\pi_{0,1}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\pi_{0,2}^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
denote the fusion product of the transgression
line bundle
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
over
(P_{0}M)^{[3]}

(see
[
Wal16
, Section 4.2]
), which provides the
bundle gerbe multiplication on
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. At a point
(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in(P_{0}M)^{[3]}
the fusion product consists of unitary isomorphisms

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{2}}\star\alpha_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{2}}\star\alpha_{0}}\ .

(4.43)

The diffeological space
(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[2]}
comes with smooth maps

\displaystyle p_{0}\colon(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[2]}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(P_{0}M)^{[3]}

(4.44)

\displaystyle\big((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\alpha_{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\overline{\gamma}\star\alpha^{\prime}_{1})

(4.45)

and

\displaystyle p_{1}\colon(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[2]}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(P_{0}M)^{[3]}

(4.46)

\displaystyle\big((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\gamma\star\alpha_{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{1},\alpha_{1})\ .

(4.47)

We set

\beta\coloneqq p_{1}^{*}\lambda^{-1}\circ r^{-1}\circ p_{0}^{*}\lambda\circ(1\otimes r)\ .

(4.48)

Explicitly, at a point
((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1}))\in(P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[2]}
, this is the
isomorphism defined by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha^{\prime}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{(\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star\gamma)\star\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{(\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star\gamma)\star\alpha_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star\alpha_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}

(4.49)

This morphism is compatible with the bundle gerbe multiplication on
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
:
consider an arbitrary point

\big((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}_{1}),(\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{0},\gamma,\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1})\big)\ \in\ (P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M)^{[3]}\ .

(4.50)

Then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\star\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha^{\prime}_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\star\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\star\alpha_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\star\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\star\alpha_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{(\alpha_{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{0})}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\lambda_{(\alpha_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{1},\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1})}}

(4.51)

The commutativity follows from the associativity of the fusion product
\lambda
and the fact that it respects the connection on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}

[
Wal16
]
so that, in particular,
\lambda
is compatible with the morphism
r
.

Construction of
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}

Next we construct the 2-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}\colon(\iota^{2*}_{1;0})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\longrightarrow(\iota^{2*}_{1,1})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}

in
\mathrm{BGrb}(P_{*}^{2}M)
that is part of the parallel transport data for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
For this, we recall that the fibre of the hermitean line bundle
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
at a loop
\gamma
is constructed from pairs
([\mathcal{S}],z)
of a 2-isomorphism class
[\mathcal{S}]
of trivialisations
\mathcal{S}\colon\gamma^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{0}
in
\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}({\mathbb{S}}^{1})
and a complex number
z\in\mathbb{C}
.
The complex line
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}
is the set of equivalence classes of such pairs under the equivalence relation

\big([\mathcal{S}],z\big)\sim\big([\mathcal{S}^{\prime}],\operatorname{hol}({\mathbb{S}}^{1},{\mathsf{R}}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{S}^{-1}))\,z\big)\ ,

(4.52)

where, for a manifold
M
, the functor
{\mathsf{R}}\colon\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)(\mathcal{I}_{B},\mathcal{I}_{B^{\prime}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{HLBdl}^{\nabla}(M)
for

B,B^{\prime}\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is essentially descent for line bundles; for
details, see
[
Bun17
,
BSS18
,
Wal07b
]

(see also Section
2.2
).

Let
M^{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}
be the diffeological space of smooth maps from the unit disk
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
to
M
.
Let

\displaystyle\partial\colon M^{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow LM

(4.53)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto f_{|{\mathbb{S}}^{1}}

(4.54)

denote the smooth map induced by restriction to the boundary of the unit disk.
The hermitean line bundle
\partial^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
on
M^{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}
has a canonical trivialisation which is defined as follows:
for a smooth map
f\colon{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\longrightarrow M
, choose a trivialisation
\mathcal{S}\colon f^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{B}
for some
B\in\Omega^{2}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
.
Define a unitary isomorphism of hermitean complex lines

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}\colon\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\partial^{*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G})_{f}

(4.55)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sigma_{f}(z)\coloneqq\Big[[\mathcal{S}_{|{\mathbb{S}}^{1}}],\exp\Big(-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}\,B\Big)\,z\Big]\ .

(4.56)

This isomorphism is defined independently of the choice of
\mathcal{S}
:
let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\colon f^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{B^{\prime}}
be another trivialisation.
Then the line bundle
{\mathsf{R}}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{S}^{-1})
has curvature
B^{\prime}-B
, which implies that

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big[[\mathcal{S}_{|{\mathbb{S}}^{1}}],\exp\Big(-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}\,B\Big)\,z\Big]

(4.57)

\displaystyle=\Big[[\mathcal{S}_{|{\mathbb{S}}^{1}}],\exp\Big(-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}\,B^{\prime}\Big)\,\operatorname{hol}\big({\mathbb{S}}^{1},{\mathsf{R}}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{S}^{-1})\big)\,z\Big]

(4.58)

\displaystyle=\Big[[\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{|{\mathbb{S}}^{1}}],\exp\Big(-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{{\mathbb{D}}^{2}}\,B^{\prime}\Big)\,z\Big]\ .

(4.59)

This construction works equally well if we replace the ‘round’ unit disk
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
by the unit square
[0,1]^{2}
, as long as we consider maps
f\colon[0,1]^{2}\longrightarrow M
whose restrictions to
\partial[0,1]^{2}
have sitting instants at the corners.
By the construction of the fusion product
\lambda
on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
, the section
\sigma
is compatible with fusion,

\sigma_{f^{\prime}\star_{2}f}=\lambda(\sigma_{f^{\prime}},\sigma_{f})

(4.60)

for all disks
f,f^{\prime}\colon[0,1]^{2}\longrightarrow M
that can be concatenated vertically.
(This is merely the statement that the integral over
[0,1]^{2}
decomposes as the sum
\int_{[0,1]^{2}}=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]}+\int_{[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{2},1]}
.)

Now consider the following setup:
let
\Sigmait\colon[0,1]^{2}\longrightarrow M
be an element in
P_{*}^{2}M
, presenting a fixed-end homotopy from a path
\gamma
to a path
\gamma^{\prime}
in
M
.
We want to compare the 1-isomorphisms
(\iota^{2*}_{1;0})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}
and
(\iota^{2*}_{1;1})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}
of bundle gerbes over
P_{*}^{2}M
.
The source bundle gerbes of both these morphisms have subductions

Y_{0}\coloneqq(\iota^{2*}_{1;0})^{*}{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}P_{0}M={\mathrm{ev}}_{(0,0)}^{*}P_{0}M={\mathrm{ev}}_{(1,0)}^{*}P_{0}M\longrightarrow P_{*}^{2}M\ ,

(4.61)

while the target bundle gerbes live over

Y_{1}\coloneqq(\iota^{2*}_{1;0})^{*}{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M={\mathrm{ev}}_{(0,1)}^{*}P_{0}M={\mathrm{ev}}_{(1,1)}^{*}P_{0}M\longrightarrow P_{*}^{2}M\ .

(4.62)

The fibre product
\widehat{Y}\coloneqq Y_{0}\times_{P_{*}^{2}M}Y_{1}
is the space of triples
(\alpha_{0},\Sigmait,\alpha_{1})
of based paths
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\in P_{0}M
and fixed-ends homotopies
\Sigmait\in P_{*}^{2}M
between arbitrary paths in
M
such that
\alpha_{t}(1)=\Sigmait(0,t)
for
t=0,1
(see Figure
2
).

\begin{overpic}[Sketch_Bigon.pdf]
\put(8.0,40.0){$\alpha_{0}$}
\put(60.0,40.0){$\alpha_{1}$}
\put(30.0,90.0){$\Sigmait$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2:
An element of the space
\widehat{Y}
.

The 1-isomorphism
(\iota^{2*}_{1;i})^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}
, for
i=0,1
, is defined over the subduction

Z_{i}\coloneqq(\iota^{2*}_{1;i})^{*}P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\longrightarrow\widehat{Y}\ ,

(4.63)

which is actually an isomorphism.
Consequently, the 2-isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}
should be defined with respect to the subduction

\widehat{Z}\coloneqq Z_{0}\times_{\widehat{Y}}Z_{1}\longrightarrow\widehat{Y}\ ,

(4.64)

which again is an isomorphism.
Its elements are triples
(\alpha_{0},\Sigmait,\alpha_{1})
as above.
Set
\gamma_{t}\coloneqq\Sigmait\circ\iota^{2}_{1;t}
for
t=0,1
, and
let
x=\gamma_{t}(0)
and
y=\gamma_{s}(1)
for
t,s=0,1
.

At a point
(\alpha_{0},\Sigmait,\alpha_{1})
, the morphism of hermitean line bundles over
\widehat{Z}
that defines
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}
is given by the morphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2|(\alpha_{0},\Sigmait,\alpha_{1})}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma_{0}\star\alpha_{0})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma_{1}\star\alpha_{0})}

(4.65)

of complex lines obtained as follows:

(1)

Using a smooth family of rotations of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, apply parallel transport on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
to obtain an isomorphism

\psi_{1}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma_{0}\star\alpha_{0})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{(\gamma_{0}\star\alpha_{0})\star\overline{\alpha_{1}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\ .

(4.66)

This is achieved by parallel transport along a thin path in
LM
.
Hence, since the parallel transport on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
is superficial, this isomorphism is independent of the choice of a smooth family of rotations.

(2)

Use the canonical section
\sigma_{\Sigmait}(1)\in\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\partial\Sigmait}
from (
4.55
) to obtain an isomorphism

\psi_{2}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\partial\Sigmait}\ .

(4.67)

(3)

The boundary loop
\partial\Sigmait
is smoothly and thinly homotopic (via reparameterisations) to
((\text{id}_{y}\star\gamma_{1})\star\text{id}_{x})\star\overline{\gamma_{0}}
, where
\text{id}_{x}
is the constant path at the point
x\in M
.
This loop is, in turn, thinly homotopic to
\gamma_{1}\star\overline{\gamma_{0}}
.
We thus obtain a canonical isomorphism

\psi_{3}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\partial\Sigmait}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{1}\star\overline{\gamma_{0}}}\ .

(4.68)

(4)

The fusion product on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
yields an isomorphism

\psi_{4}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{1}\star\overline{\gamma_{0}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{1}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\ .

(4.69)

(5)

Finally, we again use parallel transport along a path in
LM
that arises from a smooth family of rotations to obtain a canonical isomorphism

\psi_{5}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{1}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma_{1}\star\alpha_{0})}\ .

(4.70)

We then define

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2|(\alpha_{0},\Sigmait,\alpha_{1})}\coloneqq\psi_{5}\circ\psi_{4}\circ\psi_{3}\circ\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}\ .

(4.71)

This is compatible with vertical composition in
P_{*}^{2}M
:
let
\Sigmait,\Sigmait^{\prime}\in P_{*}^{2}M
be two maps
[0,1]^{2}\longrightarrow M
that can be concatenated vertically.
Since the connection on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
is superficial and compatible with the fusion product, we can replace the morphism
\psi_{1}
by

\tilde{\psi}_{1}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\iota^{2*}_{1;0}\Sigmait\star\alpha_{0})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\iota^{2*}_{1;0}\Sigmait\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\ .

(4.72)

Applying the fusion product with
\partial(\Sigmait^{\prime}\star_{2}\Sigmait)
yields an isomorphism

\varphi\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\iota^{2*}_{1;0}\Sigmait\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\iota^{2*}_{1;1}\Sigmait^{\prime}\star(\alpha_{0}\star\overline{\alpha_{1}})}\ .

(4.73)

Combining the fact that the fusion product
\lambda
is associative
and compatible with the parallel transport on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
, that the
parallel transport on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
is superficial (in particular, parallel
transport along thin paths is independent of the choice of thin path),
and that the section
\sigma
from (
4.55
) is compatible with
\lambda
, it follows that
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}
respects vertical concatenation.

Since all morphisms involved in the construction of
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}
are
smooth, it follows that
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}
is in fact a smooth morphism of bundle gerbes as desired.

Construction of
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star}

The 2-isomorphism

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star}\colon\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\longrightarrow(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,\star\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}

is directly constructed from the fusion product
\lambda
on the transgression line bundle
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
.
Define
q\colon PM\times_{M}PM\longrightarrow M
by
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\longmapsto\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
.
The morphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star}
is defined over the subduction

Q_{1}\coloneqq\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\times^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}_{q^{*}P_{0}M}\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\longrightarrow PM\times_{M}PM\ .

(4.74)

Given a point
\big((\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1}),(\alpha_{1},\gamma^{\prime},\alpha_{2})\big)\in Q_{1}
, the morphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star}
is given by
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{2}}\star(\gamma^{\prime}\star\alpha_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{(\overline{\alpha_{2}}\star\gamma^{\prime})\star\alpha_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{2}}\star(\gamma^{\prime}\star\gamma)\star\alpha_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{(\overline{\alpha_{2}}\star\gamma^{\prime})\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

(4.75)

where the horizontal morphisms are induced by smooth families of reparameterisations.
The compatibility of this morphism with the morphism
\beta
from (
4.49
) follows again from the superficiality of the connection on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
and the associativity of the fusion product
\lambda
.

The compatibility of
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star}
with
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}
as in (
4.22
) is seen analogously to how we proved the compatibility of
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}
with vertical concatenation of homotopies.
The interchange law (
4.23
) is satisfied
by the associativity of
\lambda
, its compatibility with the parallel
transport on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
and with the section
\sigma
from (
4.55
), as well as the superficiality of the connection on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
.

Construction of
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}

Finally, the 2-isomorphism

\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\colon{\mathsf{c}}^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\longrightarrow 1_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}

is obtained directly from the superficial connection on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
:
it is defined over the space of triples
(\alpha,\text{id}_{x},\alpha)\in P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M
, and all paths
of the form
\overline{\alpha}\star\alpha
are canonically contractible by thin homotopies.

All necessary coherences in Definition
4.16
then follow from the superficiality of the parallel transport on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
, the associativity of the fusion product
\lambda
and its compatibility with the section
\sigma
, and the fact that the parallel transport on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
is compatible with the fusion product.
Thus we have

Theorem 4.76
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
be a bundle gerbe with connection on
M
, and let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G})\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
be the regression of the transgression of
\mathcal{G}
.
Then the quadruple
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}=({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star},\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}})
defines a parallel transport on the bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Transfer to arbitrary bundle gerbes

In
[
Wal16
]
, Waldorf shows that the functors

\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
come with a canonical natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{A}}\colon 1\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{T}

(4.77)

as endofunctors of the homotopy 1-category
\mathtt{h}\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
.
Given a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
, we thus get a 2-isomorphism class of 1-isomorphisms
\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G})
.
Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be a representative for this class.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be a 1-isomorphism with
adjoint
inverse

{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}
, i.e. a weak inverse
{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}
together with 2-isomorphisms
\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\colon 1_{\mathcal{G}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
and
\delta_{\mathcal{B}}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\longrightarrow 1_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
that satisfy the triangle identities.
We can use it to define a 1-isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}_{1}
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}_{1}}

(4.78)

We define 2-isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1_{{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}}\circ{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{2}\circ 1_{\mathcal{B}}\ ,

(4.79)

\displaystyle{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}_{\star}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1_{{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}}\circ\big({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{\star}\circ_{2}(1_{{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}}\circ\delta_{\mathcal{B}}\circ 1_{{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}})\big)\circ 1_{\mathcal{B}}\ ,

(4.80)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\circ_{2}(1_{{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}}\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\circ 1_{\mathcal{B}})\ ,

(4.81)

where we have omitted pullbacks.
From these definitions we readily see

Proposition 4.82
.

The quadruple
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}=({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}_{1},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}_{2},{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}_{\star},\varepsilon^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}})
defines an object in
{\mathtt{PT}}(\mathcal{G})
.

For
\psi\colon{\mathcal{B}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
a 2-isomorphism of 1-isomorphisms
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}
, we obtain a 2-isomorphism

\psi^{(-1)}\circ 1_{{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}}\circ\psi\colon{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{pt}}_{1}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}}\ .

(4.83)

Here
\psi^{(-1)}
denotes the 2-isomorphism obtained from
\psi
by taking the inverse with respect to horizontal composition.
Again it follows from the definitions that this defines an isomorphism

\widehat{\psi}\colon{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}}

(4.84)

in the category
{\mathtt{PT}}(\mathcal{G})
.
If
\psi^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
is another (parallel unitary)
2-isomorphism, then
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
differ by multiplication with a locally constant
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
-valued function
f_{\psi,\psi^{\prime}}
on
M
.
Since horizontal inverses of 2-isomorphisms have dual underlying line bundles
[
Wal07b
]
, the morphisms
\psi^{(-1)}
and
\psi^{\prime}{}^{(-1)}
differ by the locally constant
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
-valued function
f_{\psi^{(-1)},\psi^{\prime}{}^{(-1)}}=(f_{\psi,\psi^{\prime}})^{-1}
.
Consequently, we deduce from (
4.83
) that

\widehat{\psi^{\prime}}=\widehat{\psi}\ .

(4.85)

That is, for any pair of parallel unitary 1-isomorphisms
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
for which there exists some parallel unitary 2-isomorphism
{\mathcal{B}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
, we obtain a
unique
isomorphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}}
.

Let
[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]
denote the full subgroupoid of
\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
on those 1-isomorphisms
\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that are isomorphic to Waldorf’s 1-isomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
Our constructions define a functor

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)}\colon[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]\longrightarrow{\mathtt{PT}}(\mathcal{G})\ .

(4.86)

This functor factors through a groupoid
[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]_{*}
with the same
objects as
[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]
and a unique isomorphism between any two
objects. In particular, every object in
[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]_{*}
is final and
the canonical functor
[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]\longrightarrow[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]_{*}
is a final
functor. It follows that, for any category
\mathscr{C}
, any
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
that factors through
[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]_{*}
has a colimit, which is represented by
\mathscr{F}({\mathcal{B}})
for any object
{\mathcal{B}}\in[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]
.

Definition 4.87
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
be a bundle gerbe with connection on
M
.
The
parallel transport
of
\mathcal{G}
is

{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}\coloneqq\operatorname{colim}\big({\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G},(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)}\colon[[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{G}}]]\longrightarrow{\mathtt{PT}}(\mathcal{G})\big)\ .

(4.88)

4.4
The transgression line bundle as a holonomy

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}^{\nabla}(M)
be a bundle gerbe with connection on
M
and write
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G})
.
We will now determine the holonomy of the parallel transport on
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
For this, consider the diffeological space
L_{*}M
of smooth maps
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\longrightarrow M
that have a sitting instant at
1\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
.
In other words,
L_{*}M
is the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{*}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}\times{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

(4.89)

in
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}fg}
, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion map and
{\Delta}
is the
diagonal embedding.
The pullback
\iota^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}
is an automorphism of
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, which we understand as the
holonomy
of
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
.
It is defined over the subduction
\iota^{*}P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\longrightarrow L_{*}M
.
Recall from Section
2.2
that a 1-automorphism of a bundle gerbe defines a line bundle via descent.
Thus the holonomy
\iota^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}
gives rise to a descended line bundle
\operatorname{hol}(\mathcal{G})\in\mathrm{HLBdl}(L_{*}M)
.
Our goal is to understand this descended line bundle more explicitly.

Let
\widehat{{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}\colon{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M\longrightarrow P_{0}M
be the morphism induced by the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{*}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}

(4.90)

in
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}fg}
. The hermitean line bundle (with connection) underlying the bundle gerbe
{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the pullback bundle

L\coloneqq\widehat{{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}^{[2]*}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow({\mathrm{ev}}_{1}^{*}P_{0}M)^{[2]}\cong\iota^{*}P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M\ .

(4.91)

We now apply the construction from the diagram (
2.29
) that produces a line bundle
{\mathsf{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
from an automorphism
{\mathcal{A}}
of a bundle gerbe:
the tensor product bundle
L^{\vee}\otimes{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}
on
\iota^{*}P_{\partial\Delta^{2}}M
has fibres

\big(L^{\vee}\otimes{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}\big)_{(\alpha_{0},\gamma,\alpha_{1})}=\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}^{\vee}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star\alpha_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\cong\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{0}}\star\alpha_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}\ .

(4.92)

Now the thin-invariant parallel transport and the fusion product on
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
yield an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{0}}\star\alpha_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star(\gamma\star\alpha_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha_{1}\star\overline{\alpha_{0}}}\otimes\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha_{0}\star(\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star\gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha_{1}\star(\overline{\alpha_{1}}\star\gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

(4.93)

By the associativity of
\lambda
, this is an isomorphism of descent data for line bundles on
L_{*}M
.
This shows

Proposition 4.94
.

The morphism (
4.93
) yields an isomorphism of hermitean line bundles

\operatorname{hol}(\mathcal{G})\coloneqq{\mathsf{R}}\big(\iota^{*}{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}_{1}\big)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}_{|L_{*}M}

(4.95)

over
L_{*}M
.

Thus the parallel transport
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
reproduces the transgression line bundle
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}
as its holonomy.

Remark 4.96
.

For a generic bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
with parallel transport
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}
, the morphism
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
induces a parallel transport on
\operatorname{hol}(\mathcal{G})
.
It should be possible to construct from this a fusion line bundle with connection on
LM
in the sense of
[
Wal16
]
, which then regresses to a bundle gerbe with connection on
M
.
Its underlying bundle gerbe should be canonically isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}

(up to 2-isomorphism), and that should allow the reconstruction of the connection on
\mathcal{G}
from its parallel transport in our sense.
However, this would go beyond the scope of this paper, and since for our applications in Sections
5
and
6
having an explicit construction for
{\mathtt{pt}}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
is sufficient, we leave this reconstruction of the connection on
\mathcal{G}
for future work.

\vartriangleleft

5
2-group extensions from bundle gerbes

Let
G
be a connected Lie group with a smooth group action on a manifold
M
.
In Section
3
we saw how a principal bundle
P\longrightarrow M
gives rise to a group extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
which encodes all information about equivariant structures on
P
.
We were able to give two equivalent constructions for
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
, one as a subgroup of
\mathrm{Diff}(P)
, and one as descent data associated to the path fibration
P_{0}G\longrightarrow G
and a parallel transport on
P
.

In this section we study the analogous situation for a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}(M)
instead of a principal bundle
P\in{\mathrm{Bun}}_{H}(M)
.
There are two main differences to the situation in Section
3
:
equivariant structures on
\mathcal{G}
form a groupoid rather than a set, and they do not assemble into a topological or smooth space.
We thus cannot expect a universal extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow G
as diffeological groups.
A good framework to describe this extension is that of group objects in categories fibred in groupoids over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, where the fibration encodes the smooth structure.
After carefully setting up this framework, we give two constructions of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
, in analogy to the two constructions of
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
in Section
3
.
We conclude this section by showing that, again, the extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow G
encodes all information about equivariant structures on
\mathcal{G}
.

5.1
Smooth groupoids and symmetries of gerbes

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}(M)
be a bundle gerbe on
M
.
Let
\Phiit\colon G\times M\longrightarrow M
be an action of a connected Lie (or
diffeological) group
G
on
M
.
Let
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
denote the category of smooth manifolds that are diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
The morphisms in
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
are the smooth maps between these manifolds.

We can view
M
and
G
as presheaves on
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
by setting

M(c)=C^{\infty}(c,M)\qquad\text{and}\qquad G(c)=C^{\infty}(c,G)\ .

(5.1)

By adding identity morphisms, we can canonically enhance the presheaf

G
to a (pre)stack on
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, i.e. a (pre)sheaf of groupoids, which we still denote by
G
.
Given a section
f\in G(c)
, i.e. a smooth map
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
, we can define a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{f}\colon c\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times 1_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit}

(5.2)

We can then assign to
f
the groupoid

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f)\coloneqq\mathrm{BGrb}(c\times M)(\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G},\Phiit_{f}^{*}\mathcal{G})\ ,

(5.3)

where
\operatorname{pr}_{M}\colon c\times M\longrightarrow M
is the projection.
The groupoid
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f)
is non-empty:
since the map
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
is homotopic to the constant map at the identity in
G
, it follows that
\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G}
and
\Phiit_{f}^{*}\mathcal{G}
have the same Dixmier-Douady class as gerbes on
c\times M
, so that there exists an isomorphism
\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\Phiit_{f}^{*}\mathcal{G}
.

The assignment
f\longmapsto{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f)
is evidently not a presheaf of groupoids on
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
since it depends not only on the object
c
, but also on a choice of a smooth map
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
.
We can reformulate this in the following way:
let
\underline{G}
denote the category with objects the smooth maps
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
, where
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
is any Cartesian space.
The morphisms

\varphi\colon(f\colon c\longrightarrow G)\longrightarrow(f^{\prime}\colon c^{\prime}\longrightarrow G)

(5.4)

in
\underline{G}
are commutative triangles of smooth maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

(5.5)

Then
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})
is a presheaf of groupoids on
\underline{G}\,
:
to an object
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
in
\underline{G}
we assign the groupoid
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f)
, while to a morphism
\varphi\colon f\longrightarrow f^{\prime}
we assign the pullback functor

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(\varphi)\coloneqq(\varphi\times 1_{M})^{*}\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f^{\prime})\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f)\ .

(5.6)

By a slight abuse of notation, we will denote the functor
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(\varphi)
by
\varphi^{*}
.
Explicitly, given a 1-isomorphism
A\colon\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\Phiit_{f^{\prime}}^{*}\mathcal{G}
over
c^{\prime}
, it is defined by the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}^{*}_{M}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{f}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\times 1_{M})^{*}\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{\prime\,\ast}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\times 1_{M})^{*}\Phiit_{f^{\prime}}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\Phiit_{f^{\prime}}\circ(\varphi\times 1_{M})\big)^{*}\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\times 1_{M})^{*}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(5.7)

By construction of the 2-category of bundle gerbes, this defines a pseudofunctor

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})\colon\underline{G}^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}\ ,

(5.8)

where
{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
is the 2-category of groupoids, functors, and natural transformations.

Remark 5.9
.

The assignment
f\longmapsto{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f)
is not a strict
presheaf of groupoids on
\underline{G}\,
, as it is only pseudofunctorial
[
Moe02
]
.
There are several ways to technically treat such pseudo-presheaves of groupoids:

(1)

Encode the coherence morphisms by viewing pseudo-presheaves of
groupoids as coherent simplicial presheaves, i.e. as simplicial
functors
\mathfrak{C}\circ{\rm N}_{\Delta}(\underline{G})^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathscr{S}et}_{\Delta}
in the notation of
[
Lur09
]
.

(2)

Use a strictification procedure to translate pseudo-presheaves of groupoids into presheaves of groupoids
[
Hol08
]
.

(3)

Use the Grothendieck construction, or straightening, to translate pseudo-presheaves of groupoids into categories fibred in groupoids over
\underline{G}

[
Vis05
,
Lur09
]
.

We will follow the third approach here because the transition between the parameterising categories
\underline{G}
and
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
becomes particularly easy in that framework.

\vartriangleleft

We will frequently make use of the Grothendieck construction to pass from
{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
-valued pseudo-functors to categories fibred in groupoids; for background on the Grothendieck construction and fibred categories we refer to
[
Vis05
,
Lur09
]
.
We will, however, describe the resulting fibred categories explicitly.
For example, the canonical projection functor
\operatorname{pr}:\underline{G}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
is the category fibred in groupoids obtained by applying the Grothendieck construction to the (pseudo)functor
c\longmapsto G(c)
, where
G(c)
is regarded as a groupoid with only identity arrows.

Definition 5.10
.

A functor
\pi\colon\mathscr{D}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
between categories is a
Grothendieck
fibration in groupoids
, or makes
\mathscr{D}
into a
category
fibred in groupoids
over
\mathscr{C}
, if it satisfies the properties:

(1)

For every object
d\in\mathscr{D}
and for every morphism
f\colon c\longrightarrow\pi(d)
in
\mathscr{C}
, there exists a morphism
\widehat{f}\colon\widehat{c}\longrightarrow d
in
\mathscr{D}
with
\pi(\widehat{f}\ )=f
.

(2)

For every pair of diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi(d_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi(d_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi(d_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f_{01}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{02}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi(\zeta_{02})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi(\zeta_{12})}

(5.11)

in
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{C}
, respectively, there exists a unique lift
\widehat{f_{01}}
of
f_{01}
that makes the upper triangle commute.

The first requirement resembles a path-lifting condition. The
second requirement can be viewed as a relative horn-filling property:
given any
\Lambda^{2}_{2}
-horn
\sigma
in
\mathscr{D}
and a filling of

\pi(\sigma)
in
\mathscr{C}
to a 2-simplex, there exists a unique filling
of
\sigma
to a 2-simplex in
\mathscr{D}
that lifts the 2-simplex in

\mathscr{C}
. Alternatively, consider an arbitrary functor
\pi\colon\mathscr{D}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
between categories and a morphism
\zeta_{12}\colon d_{1}\longrightarrow d_{2}
in
\mathscr{D}
.
If for every pair of solid arrow diagrams as in (
5.11
) the dashed arrow exists such that the upper triangle commutes and such that
\pi(\widehat{f_{01}})=f_{01}
, one says that
\zeta_{12}
is
\pi
-Cartesian
.
In particular, if
\pi
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, then property (2) of Definition
5.10
is equivalent to saying that every morphism in
\mathscr{D}
is
\pi
-Cartesian.
If
\pi\colon\mathscr{D}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a Grothendieck fibration in
groupoids and
c\in\mathscr{C}
, we denote by
\mathscr{D}_{|c}=\pi^{-1}(c)
the
fibre over
c
, which is the groupoid with objects
d\in\mathscr{D}
such
that
\pi(d)=c
and morphisms
\widehat{f}\colon d\longrightarrow d^{\prime}
such
that
\pi(\widehat{f}\ )=1_{c}
.

Definition 5.12
.

A category fibred in groupoids over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
is a
smooth groupoid
.
Let
\mathscr{H}
denote the strict 2-category of smooth groupoids.
Its objects are smooth groupoids, its morphisms are (strictly) commutative diagrams of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{X}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{X}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}}

(5.13)

and its 2-morphisms are natural transformations that project to the identity.
We denote by
\underline{\mathscr{H}}({\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{Z}})
the groupoid of functors
{\mathsf{X}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{Z}}
that project to the identity on
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.

Example 5.14
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold.
An important example of a smooth groupoid is given by the Grothendieck fibration
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, whose objects are pairs
(c,L)
of a Cartesian space
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
and a hermitean line bundle
L\longrightarrow c\times M
, and whose morphisms
(c,L)\longrightarrow(c^{\prime},L^{\prime})
are pairs
(\varphi,\psi)
of a smooth map
\varphi\colon c\longrightarrow c^{\prime}
and an isomorphism
\psi\colon L\longrightarrow(\varphi\times 1_{M})^{*}L^{\prime}
of hermitean line bundles on
c\times M
.
One can interpret
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
as describing smooth families of hermitean line bundles on
M
.
For
M=*
, we write
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{*}\eqqcolon\mathrm{HLBdl}
.

\vartriangleleft

Definition 5.15
.

Let
p\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\underline{G}
denote the category fibred in groupoids obtained by applying the Grothendieck construction to the pseudofunctor
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})\colon\underline{G}^{\text{op}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
.
Explicitly, the category
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
consists of:

•

\underline{\rm Objects:}
pairs
(f,A)
, where
f\in\underline{G}
is a smooth map
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
and
A\colon\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\Phiit_{f}^{*}\mathcal{G}
is a 1-isomorphism of bundle gerbes over
c\times M
.

•

\underline{\rm Morphisms:}
a morphism
(f_{0},A_{0})\longrightarrow(f_{1},A_{1})
is a pair
(\varphi,\psi)
of a morphism
\varphi\colon f_{0}\longrightarrow f_{1}
in
\underline{G}
and a 2-isomorphism
\psi\colon A_{0}\longrightarrow\varphi^{*}A_{1}
in
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}^{{\mathtt{PSh}}}(\mathcal{G})(f_{0})
.

The functor
p\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\underline{G}
is automatically a fibration in groupoids, since it arises as the Grothendieck construction of a pseudo-presheaf of groupoids.
Since Grothendieck fibrations are stable under composition
[
Vis05
]
, the composite functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}

(5.16)

makes
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
into a smooth groupoid.

5.2
Smooth 2-groups

We would now like to establish that
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
is not just a smooth groupoid, but can also be regarded as a higher group in some sense.
That is, we would like to find on
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
the same type of structure as we found on the bundle
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G
in Section
3.1
.
Here, however, we are working inside the ambient 2-category
\mathscr{H}
, and so we will need to make precise what we mean by a group in
\mathscr{H}
.
The notion of a group object in a 2-category goes back to
[
BL04
]
.
The following definitions are taken from
[
SP11
]
which are strongly based on
[
BL04
]
.
Let
\mathscr{C}
be a 2-category with finite products; in particular, it has a terminal object
*
.
Examples are the 2-categories
{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
and
\mathscr{H}
.

Definition 5.17

(
[
BL04
]
)
.

A
monoid object
in
\mathscr{C}
is a sextuple
({\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{u}},\otimes,{\mathsf{a}},{\mathsf{l}},{\mathsf{r}})
of

•

an object
{\mathsf{H}}\in\mathscr{C}
,

•

1-morphisms
{\mathsf{u}}\colon*\longrightarrow{\mathsf{H}}
and
\otimes\colon{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{H}}
, and

•

2-isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\mathsf{a}}\colon\otimes\circ\,(\otimes\times 1_{\mathsf{H}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\otimes\circ(1_{\mathsf{H}}\times\otimes)\ ,

(5.18)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{l}}\colon\otimes\circ\,({\mathsf{u}}\times 1_{\mathsf{H}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 1_{\mathsf{H}}\ ,

(5.19)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{r}}\colon\otimes\circ\,(1_{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{u}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 1_{\mathsf{H}}\ .

(5.20)

These data are required to satisfy a pentagon and a triangle identity; see
[
SP11
, Definition 41]
.

An
abelian monoid object
comes with an additional 2-isomorphism
\beta\colon\otimes\circ\,\tau\longrightarrow\otimes
satisfying the coherence conditions in
[
SP11
, Definition 47]
, where
\tau\colon{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{H}}
is the interchange of factors.

Definition 5.21
.

A
1-morphism
of monoid objects
({\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{u}},\otimes,{\mathsf{a}},{\mathsf{l}},{\mathsf{r}})\longrightarrow({\mathsf{H}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{u}}^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime},{\mathsf{a}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{l}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{r}}^{\prime})
in
\mathscr{C}
consists of a triple
(F_{1},F_{\otimes},F_{\mathsf{u}})
of

•

a 1-morphism
F_{1}\colon{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{H}}^{\prime}
and

•

2-isomorphisms
F_{\otimes}\colon\otimes^{\prime}\circ\,(F_{1}\times F_{1})\longrightarrow F_{1}\circ\otimes
and
F_{\mathsf{u}}\colon{\mathsf{u}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow F_{1}\circ{\mathsf{u}}
.

These are required to satisfy the coherence conditions in
[
SP11
, Definition 42]
.

Morphisms of abelian monoid objects satisfy an additional compatibility condition for the symmetries
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
, which can be found in
[
SP11
, Definition 48]
.

Definition 5.22

(
[
SP11
, Definition 43]
)
.

A
2-morphism

(F_{1},F_{\otimes},F_{\mathsf{u}})\longrightarrow(E_{1},E_{\otimes},E_{\mathsf{u}})
of monoid objects in
\mathscr{C}
is a 2-morphism
\theta\colon F_{1}\longrightarrow E_{1}
such that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{\prime}\circ(F_{1}\times F_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{\prime}\circ(E_{1}\times E_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}\circ\otimes}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}\circ\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{\prime}\circ(\theta\times\theta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\circ\otimes}

(5.23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}\circ{\mathsf{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}\circ{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\circ{\mathsf{u}}}

(5.24)

commute.
2-morphisms of abelian monoid objects are 2-morphisms of the underlying monoid objects.

Example 5.25
.

A monoid object in the 2-category
\operatorname{\mathscr{C}at}
of categories is precisely a monoidal category.
Similarly, 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms between monoid objects in
\operatorname{\mathscr{C}at}
are precisely the monoidal functors and the monoidal natural transformations, respectively.
The abelian monoids in
\operatorname{\mathscr{C}at}
are precisely the symmetric monoidal categories.

\vartriangleleft

Definition 5.26

(
[
SP11
, Definition 41]
)
.

A
group object
in
\mathscr{C}
is a monoid object
({\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{u}},\otimes,{\mathsf{a}},{\mathsf{l}},{\mathsf{r}})
in
\mathscr{C}
such that the 1-morphism

(\otimes,\operatorname{pr}_{1})\colon{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{H}}

(5.27)

is (weakly) invertible.
An
abelian group object
in
\mathscr{C}
is an abelian monoid object whose underlying monoid object is a group object.

For
\mathscr{C}
a 2-category with finite products, we denote the 2-category of group objects in
\mathscr{C}
by
2{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rp}}(\mathscr{C})
.

Definition 5.28
.

The 2-category of
2-groups
is
2{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rp}}({\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}})
.
The 2-category of
smooth 2-groups
is
2{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rp}}(\mathscr{H})
.

Both these 2-categories are enriched in groupoids.
Let us examine Definition
5.28
a little more closely.
Consider two objects
\pi_{\mathsf{C}}\colon{\mathsf{C}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{D}}\colon{\mathsf{D}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
in
\mathscr{H}
.
The product in
\mathscr{H}
is given by the pullback in
\operatorname{\mathscr{C}at}
:

\big({\mathsf{C}}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{\mathsf{C}}\ }{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}\big)\times\big({\mathsf{D}}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{\mathsf{D}}\ }{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}\big)=\big({\mathsf{C}}\times_{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}{\mathsf{D}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}\big)\ .

(5.29)

Explicitly, the category
{\mathsf{C}}\times_{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}{\mathsf{D}}
has

•

\underline{\rm Objects:}
pairs
(c\in{\mathsf{C}},d\in{\mathsf{D}})
such that
\pi_{\mathsf{C}}(c)=\pi_{\mathsf{D}}(d)
.

•

\underline{\rm Morphisms:}
pairs
(\phi,\psi)
of morphisms
\phi
in
{\mathsf{C}}
and
\psi
in
{\mathsf{D}}
such that
\pi_{\mathsf{C}}(\phi)=\pi_{\mathsf{D}}(\psi)
.

A monoid structure on
{\mathsf{C}}\in\mathscr{H}
thus allows us to multiply pairs of objects
in the same fibre
and pairs of morphisms that lie
over the same morphism
in
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.

Example 5.30
.

The tensor product of line bundles turns the presheaf of groupoids of hermitean line
bundles with connection
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{\nabla}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
into an abelian group object in
\mathscr{H}
.
Similarly, for any manifold
M
it also turns the internal hom

\big(\mathrm{HLBdl}^{\nabla}\big)^{M}
into an abelian group object in
\mathscr{H}
.

\vartriangleleft

5.3
Smooth principal 2-bundles

We shall now establish our precise notion of an extension of smooth 2-groups.

Definition 5.31
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a 2-category with finite products, let
({\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{u}},\otimes_{\mathsf{H}},{\mathsf{a}},{\mathsf{l}},{\mathsf{r}})
be a monoid object in
\mathscr{C}
, and let
{\mathsf{C}}\in\mathscr{C}
.
A
right action
of
{\mathsf{H}}
on
{\mathsf{C}}
is a morphism
\otimes\colon{\mathsf{C}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{C}}
in
\mathscr{C}
, together with
2-morphisms
\alpha
and
u
in
\mathscr{C}
that witness the commutativity of the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathsf{C}}\times\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\times 1_{\mathsf{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathsf{C}}\times{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

(5.32)

and that are coherent with respect to the 2-isomorphism
{\mathsf{a}}
,
{\mathsf{l}}

and
{\mathsf{r}}
.
Left actions are defined analogously.

Example 5.33
.

The standard example for an action of a monoid object is that of a module category
{\mathsf{C}}
over a monoidal category
{\mathsf{H}}
in
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{\mathscr{C}at}
.

\vartriangleleft

Definition 5.34
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category.
Suppose there are categories fibred in groupoids
\pi_{i}\colon{\mathsf{D}}_{i}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
, for
i=0,1
, and
\pi_{\mathsf{E}}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
over

\mathscr{C}
, and suppose there is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}

(5.35)

of categories fibred in groupoids over
\mathscr{C}
.
The
homotopy pullback

{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\mathtt{h}}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
is the category with

•

\underline{\rm Objects:}
triples
(d_{0},\eta,d_{1})
, where
d_{i}\in{\mathsf{D}}_{i}
and
\eta\colon F_{0}(d_{0})\longrightarrow F_{1}(d_{1})
is an isomorphism in
{\mathsf{E}}
that projects to the identity under
\pi_{\mathsf{E}}
.

•

\underline{\rm Morphisms:}
a morphism
(d_{0},\eta,d_{1})\longrightarrow(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})
is a pair
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
of morphisms
\psi_{i}\colon d_{i}\longrightarrow d^{\prime}_{i}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(d_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(d_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(d^{\prime}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(d^{\prime}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(\psi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(\psi_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}

(5.36)

commutes in
{\mathsf{E}}
.

This comes with a canonical functor

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathtt{h}}\colon{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\mathtt{h}}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}

(5.37)

\displaystyle(d_{0},\eta,d_{1})

\displaystyle\longmapsto\pi_{0}(d_{0})=\pi_{1}(d_{1})

(5.38)

\displaystyle(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})

\displaystyle\longmapsto\pi_{0}(\psi_{0})=\pi_{1}(\psi_{1})\ ,

(5.39)

which, as we show in Appendix
A
, is a
Grothendieck fibration in groupoids.

Definition 5.40
.

Let
{\mathsf{H}}
be a smooth 2-group, and let
{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathscr{H}
be any smooth groupoid.
An
{\mathsf{H}}
-principal 2-bundle
on
{\mathsf{X}}
is an object
{\mathsf{P}}\in\mathscr{H}
with a morphism
\pi\colon{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}
, a right action
(\otimes,\alpha)
of
{\mathsf{H}}
on
{\mathsf{P}}
and a 2-isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

(5.41)

such that

(1)

the functor
\pi\colon{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}
is an essentially
surjective Grothendieck fibration,

(2)

the action
(\otimes,\alpha)
of
{\mathsf{H}}
on
{\mathsf{P}}
and the
2-isomorphism
\eta
are compatible in the sense that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathsf{P}}\times\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\times 1_{\mathsf{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}

(5.42)

is coherent, where the front face carries the 2-isomorphism
\alpha
that is part of the action of
{\mathsf{H}}
on
{\mathsf{P}}
, the back, right-hand, and bottom faces carry the 2-isomorphism
\eta
, and the left-hand face commutes strictly,

(3)

the composition
{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\mathsf{X}}{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathtt{h}}{\mathsf{P}}
is an equivalence, where the first functor is
induced by the diagonal functor
{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\mathsf{X}}{\mathsf{P}}
.

The first condition can be understood as demanding that
{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}
has local sections (see Lemma
A.1
from Appendix
A
).
The second condition implements the property that the
{\mathsf{H}}
-action preserves the projection to
{\mathsf{X}}
up to coherent homotopy.
The third condition says that the
{\mathsf{H}}
-action is principal.
Note that upon choosing an inverse to the equivalence
{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\mathsf{X}}{\mathsf{P}}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathtt{h}}{\mathsf{P}}
,
one could equivalently formulate condition (3) using strict pullbacks
alone (again by Lemma
A.1
from
Appendix
A
).

In order to understand the notion of an extension of smooth 2-groups, we first need to define the kernel of a morphism of smooth 2-groups.
Naively, the kernel could easily be defined as a fibre over
{\mathsf{u}}
, but the resulting category will not generally be fibred in groupoids over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.
As it turns out, the homotopy pullback does satisfy this property.

Definition 5.43
.

Let
p\colon{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}
be a morphism of smooth 2-groups in
\mathscr{H}
.
Its
kernel

\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
is the homotopy pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}=*_{\mathscr{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{G}}}

(5.44)

Explicitly,
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
is given by

\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)\coloneqq*_{\mathscr{H}}\times_{\mathsf{G}}^{\mathtt{h}}{\mathsf{H}}\ .

(5.45)

Using Definition
5.34
we can equivalently describe it as the category with objects given by pairs
(h,\eta)
of an object
h\in{\mathsf{H}}
and an isomorphism
\eta\colon p(h)\longrightarrow{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{G}}(\pi_{\mathsf{H}}(h))
in
{\mathsf{G}}
.
Its morphisms
(h_{0},\eta_{0})\longrightarrow(h_{1},\eta_{1})
are given by morphisms
\zeta\colon h_{0}\longrightarrow h_{1}
such that
\eta_{1}\circ p(\zeta)=u_{\mathsf{G}}(\pi_{\mathsf{H}}(\zeta))\circ\eta_{0}
.
We readily observe that the restrictions of the structure morphisms

\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}
,
{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathsf{H}}
,
{\mathsf{l}}_{\mathsf{H}}
and
{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathsf{H}}
, together with the morphism
{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{H}}
, turn
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
into a smooth 2-group.
It should also be possible to turn the strict kernel
\ker(p)
into a smooth 2-group in this case, using an inverse to the equivalence
\ker(p)\hookrightarrow\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
(compare Lemma
A.2
from
Appendix
A
), but the homotopy-kernel

\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
carries a
canonical
2-group structure,
and using
\ker(p)
instead of
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
would make Construction
5.48
below rather cumbersome.

Lemma 5.46
.

In the setting of Definition
5.43
, the functor
\kappa

is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids.

Proof.

This follows directly by Lemma
A.2
(1) from Appendix
A
.
∎

Let
\ker(p)
denote the strict pullback of the diagram
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}\overset{{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{G}}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathsf{G}}\overset{p}{\longleftarrow}{\mathsf{H}}
.
Explicitly, it is the category with objects
h\in{\mathsf{H}}
such that

p(h)={\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{G}}(\pi_{\mathsf{H}}(h))
and morphisms
\zeta\colon h_{0}\longrightarrow h_{1}

such that
p(\zeta)={\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{G}}(\pi_{\mathsf{H}}(\zeta))
.
The functor
\ker(p)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
is not a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids in general.
However, if
p\colon{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, then so are the functors
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
and
\ker(p)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, and
the canonical inclusion
\ker(p)\hookrightarrow\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
is an equivalence.
The next definition is loosely modelled on
[
SP11
, Definition 75]
.

Definition 5.47
.

Let
{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\mathsf{G}}
be smooth 2-groups.
An
extension
of
{\mathsf{G}}
by
{\mathsf{A}}
is a pair
(F,p)
of a morphism of smooth 2-groups
p\colon{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}
that turns
{\mathsf{H}}
into a
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
-principal 2-bundle over
{\mathsf{G}}
, and an equivalence of smooth 2-groups
F\colon{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
.

By Lemma
A.2
from Appendix
A
, we could equivalently require
p
to turn
{\mathsf{H}}
into a
\ker(p)
-principal 2-bundle, but then we would need to use the non-canonical 2-group structure on
\ker(p)
.
This essentially amounts to choosing an inverse for the equivalence

\ker(p)\hookrightarrow\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
.

Our goal now is to define when an extension of smooth 2-groups is central.
Again, we follow the ideas of
[
SP11
]
, where the criterion for an extension of
{\mathsf{G}}
by
{\mathsf{A}}
to be central is formulated using a functor
{\mathsf{G}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Aut}({\mathsf{A}})
from
{\mathsf{G}}
into the automorphisms of
{\mathsf{A}}
as a 2-group; the smooth structure does not matter here.
In
[
SP11
]
, this functor is obtained from abstract arguments.

Construction 5.48
.

In our formalism, we can understand this construction as follows:
consider smooth 2-groups
{\mathsf{G}}
and
{\mathsf{A}}
, where
{\mathsf{A}}
is abelian,
and let
(F,p)
be a smooth 2-group extension of
{\mathsf{G}}
by
{\mathsf{A}}
,
with morphism
p\colon{\mathsf{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}
.
Then
{\mathsf{A}}
is abelian if and only if
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
is
abelian, which is true if and only if
\ker(p)
is abelian (since the 2-group structure induces Picard groupoid structures on the fibres of these smooth 2-groups, where
F
induces monoidal equivalences).
Fix an arbitrary Cartesian space
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.
Let
\mathsf{Aut}(\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c})
denote the Picard groupoid of monoidal autoequivalences of the fibre
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c}
of
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
over
c
.
Note that we do not claim that the Picard groupoids
\mathsf{Aut}(\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c})
assemble into a smooth 2-group (though it might be possible to achieve this).
We claim that there is a functor
{\mathsf{G}}_{|c}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Aut}(\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c})
which is canonical up to unique natural isomorphism.

Let
(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)^{\vee}\colon{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}
denote a choice of functorial inverse in
{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}
.
This can always be enhanced to a functorial choice of
adjoint inverse
, i.e. a functor
k\longmapsto(k^{\vee},{\mathrm{ev}}_{k},{\mathrm{coev}}_{k})
that maps
k
to a triple of a dual object
k^{\vee}
, and duality morphisms (which are isomorphisms in this case)
{\mathrm{ev}}_{k}\colon k\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}k^{\vee}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{H}}(c)
and
{\mathrm{coev}}_{k}\colon{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{H}}(c)\longrightarrow k^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}k
which satisfy the triangle identities.
The functor
(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)^{\vee}
acts on morphisms
\psi\colon k\longrightarrow k^{\prime}
by taking the dual of
\psi^{-1}
with respect to the chosen duality data on
k
and
k^{\vee}
.
This enhancement can be achieved by choosing an
adjoint inverse
for the equivalence of categories
(\otimes_{\mathsf{H}},\operatorname{pr}_{1})_{|c}\colon{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}\times{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}\times{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}
(which is always possible).

To an object
k\in{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}
we associate the functor

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ad}}_{k}\colon\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c}

(5.49)

\displaystyle(h,\varphi)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(k\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}h\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}k^{\vee},\varphi_{k})

(5.50)

\displaystyle\big(\zeta\colon(h_{0},\varphi_{0})\longrightarrow(h_{1},\varphi_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1_{k}\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}\zeta\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}1_{k^{\vee}}\ ,

(5.51)

where the morphism
\varphi_{k}
is the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(k\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}h\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}k^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(k)\otimes_{\mathsf{G}}p(h)\otimes_{\mathsf{G}}p(k^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{G}}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(k\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}k^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(k)\otimes_{\mathsf{G}}p(k^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(k)\otimes_{\mathsf{G}}{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{G}}(c)\otimes_{\mathsf{G}}p(k^{\vee})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{p(k)}\otimes_{\mathsf{G}}\varphi\otimes_{\mathsf{G}}1_{p(k^{\vee})}}

(5.52)

Given another object
k^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}
such that
p(k)=p(k^{\prime})
in
{\mathsf{G}}
, the principality condition implies that there exists an object
(b,\beta)\in\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
and an isomorphism
\psi\colon k^{\prime}\longrightarrow k\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}b
.
Since
\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)
is abelian, this induces an isomorphism

\alpha_{k,k^{\prime}}\colon(1_{k}\times{\mathrm{ev}}_{b}\times 1_{k^{\vee}})\circ(\psi\times 1_{h}\times\psi^{\vee})\colon{\mathrm{Ad}}_{k^{\prime}}(h)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Ad}}_{k}(h)\ .

(5.53)

By the functoriality of
(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)^{\vee}
, any other choice of
(b,\beta)
and
\psi
yields the same isomorphism
{\mathrm{Ad}}_{k^{\prime}}(h)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Ad}}_{k}(h)
in this way.
Furthermore, this isomorphism is natural in
k
and
h
by the functoriality of
\otimes_{\mathsf{H}}
and
(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,)^{\vee}
.
That is, the pair
({\mathrm{Ad}},\alpha)
defines an object

({\mathrm{Ad}},\alpha)_{c}\in\operatorname{holim}^{{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}}\underline{2{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rp}}}\big({\mathsf{H}}_{|c}^{[\bullet]},\mathsf{Aut}(\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c})\big)\ ,

(5.54)

where
{\mathsf{H}}_{|c}^{[\bullet]}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}_{|c}
denotes the Čech nerve of the functor
p_{|c}
.
As we show in the proof of Proposition
A.7
in Appendix
A
, any choice of preimages of the objects
g\in{\mathsf{G}}_{|c}
under
p_{|c}
now induces a functor
{\mathsf{G}}_{|c}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Aut}(\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c})
from these data.
Moreover, any other choice of such preimages will induce a canonical natural isomorphism of functors.
Hence we obtain a well-defined isomorphism class of functors, which we denote by

[{\mathrm{Ad}},\alpha]_{c}\in\pi_{0}\Big(\underline{2{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rp}}}\big({\mathsf{G}}_{|c},\mathsf{Aut}(\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c})\big)\Big)\ .

(5.55)

This class allows us to state when a smooth 2-group extension is central.

\vartriangleleft

Definition 5.56
.

Let
(F,p)
be an extension of a smooth 2-group
{\mathsf{G}}
by a smooth 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
.
Then
(F,p)
is
central
if
{\mathsf{A}}
is abelian, and for every
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
the isomorphism class
[{\mathrm{Ad}},\alpha]_{c}
agrees with the
isomorphism class of the trivial 2-group morphism
{\mathsf{G}}_{|c}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Aut}(\ker^{\mathtt{h}}(p)_{|c})
.

5.4
Global description of the 2-group extension

We shall now apply the general considerations of
Sections
5.2

and
5.3
to the smooth groupoid

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
constructed in Section
5.1
.

Theorem 5.57
.

The functor
\pi\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
is a smooth 2-group.

Proof.

First we show that
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
carries the structure of a monoid object in
\mathscr{H}
.
The terminal object
*_{\mathscr{H}}\in\mathscr{H}
is the identity functor
1_{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}\colon{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.
We start by defining the 1-morphism
{\mathsf{u}}\colon*_{\mathscr{H}}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
; it is the functor that assigns to every object
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
the object
(e_{c},\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}1_{\mathcal{G}})\in{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
, where
e_{c}\colon c\longrightarrow G
is the constant map at the identity object
e\in G
.

Next we define the 1-morphism

\otimes\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\times_{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})

in the following way:
consider two arbitrary objects
(f_{0},A_{0}),(f_{1},A_{1})\in{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
in
the same fibre of
\pi\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, i.e.
f_{0},f_{1}
are defined over the same object
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.
We define the map
(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\times c\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\times G\times M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times f_{0}\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\Phiit}

(5.58)

Observe that

\operatorname{pr}_{M}\circ(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})=\Phiit_{f_{0}}\ ,\quad\Phiit_{f_{1}}\circ(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})=\Phiit_{f_{1}\,f_{0}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(1,\Phiit_{f_{1}})\circ(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})=(1,\Phiit_{f_{1}\,f_{0}})\ ,

(5.59)

where the second and third identities use the fact that
\Phiit
is a group action.
Thus we can form the 1-morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{f_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})^{*}\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phiit_{f_{1}\,f_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})^{*}\Phiit_{f_{1}}^{*}\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})^{*}A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(5.60)

The solid unlabelled arrows are canonical isomorphisms that stem from
the fact that
\mathrm{BGrb}
is a (pre)sheaf of 2-categories on the category
of manifolds
\operatorname{\mathscr{M}fd}

[
Wal07b
,
NS11
]
.
By a slight abuse of notation, we denote the composite morphism by
(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})^{*}A_{1}\circ A_{0}
.
Then we set

(f_{1},A_{1})\otimes(f_{0},A_{0})\coloneqq\big(f_{1}\,f_{0},\,(1,\Phiit_{f_{0}})^{*}A_{1}\circ A_{0}\big)\ ,

(5.61)

and analogously on 2-isomorphisms.
The associator and unitors are readily obtained from those in the sheaf of 2-categories
\mathrm{BGrb}
.
The coherence conditions in
\mathrm{BGrb}
imply that
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
, endowed with the multiplication and coherence morphisms defined here, is a monoid object in
\mathscr{H}
.

Now we show that
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
is in fact a group object in
\mathscr{H}
.
Set

(f,A)^{-1}\coloneqq\big(f^{-1},\,(1,\Phiit_{f^{-1}})^{*}A^{-1}\big)

(5.62)

and analogously on morphisms, where
f^{-1}
denotes the composition
of the map
f\colon c\longrightarrow G
with the inversion map in the group
G
.
