\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}\log f_{i,j}(x)

\displaystyle=A_{i+j}(x)-A_{i}(x)-A_{j}(x+i)\ ,

(6.35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}A

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}^{*}B-\pi_{0}^{*}B\ ,

(6.36)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. The second condition implies that
the closed 3-form
\mathrm{d}B=\pi^{*}H
descends to a well-defined flux
H
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
Using the connection we can construct a section

s_{A,B}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}\longrightarrow\text{Sym}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}}(\mathcal{G}_{f})

by

\displaystyle s_{A,B}(v)\coloneqq(g_{i},v)\qquad\mbox{with}\quad g_{i}=\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{1}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v)}\,A_{i}\Big)\ .

(6.37)

We check that this is indeed an element
of
\text{Sym}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}}(\mathcal{G}_{f})_{|v}
:

\displaystyle g_{i}(x)\,g_{j}(x+i)\,g_{i+j}(x)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{1}(x;v)}\,A_{i}+\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{1}(x+i;v)}\,A_{j}-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{1}(x;v)}\,A_{i+j}\Big)

(6.38)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\int_{\Delta^{1}(x;v)}\,\mathrm{d}\log f_{i,j}\Big)

(6.39)

\displaystyle=f_{i,j}(x-v)^{-1}\,f_{i,j}(x)\ .

(6.40)

For the multiplication we find

\displaystyle s_{A,B}(v)\otimes s_{A,B}(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigg(\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{1}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,-v^{\prime};v)}\,A_{i}+\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{1}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime})}\,A_{i}\Big)\,,\,v+v^{\prime}\bigg)

(6.41)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{1}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v+v^{\prime})}\,A_{i}-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{2}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime},v)}\,\big(B-\tau_{i}^{*}B\big)\Big)\,,\,v+v^{\prime}\bigg)

(6.42)

\displaystyle=s_{A,B}(v+v^{\prime})\otimes\bigg(\exp\Big(-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{2}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime},v)}\big(B-\tau_{i}^{*}B\big)\Big)\,,\,0\bigg)\ .

(6.43)

This particular product is associative on the nose. However, the line
bundle on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}

described by the transition functions

\exp\big(-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{2}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime},v)}\,(B-\tau_{i}^{*}B)\big)
is non-trivial. We can use the decomposition

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta^{2}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime},v)}\,\mathrm{d}\imath_{\bullet}B=\pounds_{\bullet}\int_{\Delta^{2}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime},v)}\,B-\int_{\Delta^{2}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime},v)}\,\imath_{\bullet}\pi^{*}H\ ,

(6.44)

where
\pounds
is the Lie derivative,
to construct a 2-isomorphism

\displaystyle\Piit_{v,v^{\prime}}\coloneqq\exp\Big(-\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{2}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;v^{\prime},v)}\,B\Big)\colon s_{A,B}(v)\otimes s_{A,B}(v^{\prime})\longrightarrow s_{A,B}(v+v^{\prime})\ ,

(6.45)

which has the advantage that the line bundle on the right-hand side is
trivial.

Remark 6.46
.

In this last representation the nonassociativity of the higher magnetic translations is realised by the
composition property

\displaystyle\Piit_{u,v+w}\circ\tau_{-u}^{*}\Piit_{v,w}=\omega_{u,v,w}\ \Piit_{u+v,w}\circ\Piit_{u,v}\ ,

(6.47)

where

\displaystyle\omega_{u,v,w}=\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\,\int_{\Delta^{3}(\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,;w,v,u)}\,\pi^{*}H\Big)

(6.48)

and

\displaystyle\Delta^{3}(x;w,v,u)=\{x-u-v-w+t_{1}\,w+t_{2}\,v+t_{3}\,u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid 0\leq t_{3}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{1}\leq 1\}\ .

(6.49)

Concretely this means that there are two different ways to go from the
triple product

s_{A,B}(u)\otimes s_{A,B}(v)\otimes s_{A,B}(w)
to
s_{A,B}(u+v+w)
.
Their difference is controlled by the 3-cocycle

\omega_{\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,}

on the translation group
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathtt{t}}
with values in

C^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d};{\operatorname{U}}(1))
, as depicted in the
commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A,B}(u)\otimes s_{A,B}(v+w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A,B}(u)\otimes\big(s_{A,B}(v)\otimes s_{A,B}(w)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A,B}(u+v+w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(s_{A,B}(u)\otimes s_{A,B}(v)\big)\otimes s_{A,B}(w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A,B}(u+v+w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A,B}(u+v)\otimes s_{A,B}(w)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Piit_{u,v+w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\Piit_{v,w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{u,v,w}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Piit_{u,v}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Piit_{u+v,w}}

(6.50)

This is the implementation of nonassociativity in the higher categorical framework.

\vartriangleleft

7
Application II: Anomalies in quantum field theory

In this section we begin by using the group extensions
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
from Section
3

to study the existence of equivariant trivialisations of line bundles.
This has direct applications to the path integral description of the
chiral anomaly in quantum field theory on even-dimensional spacetime manifolds.
Then using the 2-group extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
from
Section
5
, we study
the analogous questions
for gerbes and apply our findings to the Hamiltonian description
of the chiral anomaly on odd-dimensional time-slices.

7.1
Even dimensions: Chiral anomalies

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
M
a manifold with smooth
G
-action
\Phiit\colon G\times M\longrightarrow M
, and

(P,\chi)
a
G
-equivariant
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
-bundle on
M
.
The equivariant structure on
P
can be described by a splitting

s_{P}\colon G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
. Assume furthermore that
P
is
trivial as a line bundle, i.e. there exists a
1-isomorphism
\psi\colon I\longrightarrow P
.
The trivial bundle carries a canonical equivariant structure with corresponding splitting
s_{I}\colon G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(I)
.

Rewriting the result of Section
3.2
slightly, we see that
\psi
is equivariant if and only if
the smooth 1-cocycle

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M;{\operatorname{U}}(1))

(7.1)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\longmapsto s_{P}(g)\,\widehat{\psi}\big(s_{I}(g)\big)^{-1}

(7.2)

is trivial. Every other isomorphism
I\longrightarrow P

differs from
\psi
by a uniquely determined element of
C^{\infty}(M;{\operatorname{U}}(1))
. Their corresponding 1-cocycles
differ by the coboundary defined by this element. Hence the obstruction for an equivariant bundle which is trivial
as a line bundle to be also trivial as an equivariant bundle is an
element of the degree one group cohomology
{\rm H}^{1}\big(G;C^{\infty}(M;{\operatorname{U}}(1))\big)
.
This has also been observed in
[
CM95
]
from a different perspective.

The question of whether a bundle is equivariantly trivial
is important in the path integral perspective on
chiral anomalies in quantum field theory. Let
M
be a based
even-dimensional compact Riemannian spin manifold,
G
a
Lie group,

Q
a principal
G
-bundle on
M
, and
\rho\colon G\longrightarrow\mathsf{End}(V)

a unitary representation of
G
on a finite-dimensional vector space

V
which encodes the matter content of the field theory.
Denote by
S^{+}
and
S^{-}
the positive and negative chirality spinor
bundles on
M
,
respectively, by
\Gamma
the group of based gauge transformations of
Q
and
by
\mathscr{A}
the affine space of connections on
Q
.
The field content of the theory are chiral spinors, which are smooth sections of the vector bundle

S^{+}\otimes V
, where here
V
is the hermitean vector bundle associated to
Q
via the
representation
\rho
.
There is a
family of (twisted) Dirac operators

{D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A}\colon\Gamma(M;S^{+}\otimes V)\longrightarrow\Gamma(M;S^{-}\otimes V)

parameterised by gauge fields
A\in\mathscr{A}
, which are first
order elliptic differential operators acting on chiral spinors. These data
together define the content of a
chiral gauge theory
.

The formal path integral over the chiral spinor fields is the
determinant
\det({D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A})
of the Dirac operator
{D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A}
.
However, the determinant of
{D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A}
is in general not a number but an
element of a complex
line, and it
can be defined only after suitable
regularisation as an element of the fibre of the determinant line bundle

\det\longrightarrow\mathscr{A}

[
AS84
]
. This defines the (exponentiated)
effective action functional
which is a section

\displaystyle Z=\exp(-S):{\mathscr{A}}\longrightarrow\det\ ,

(7.3)

with
S(A)=-\log\,\det({D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A})
.
The action of
\Gamma
on
\mathscr{A}
via gauge transformations
can be lifted to the determinant line bundle, which hence descends to a
line bundle over the moduli space of gauge connections
\mathscr{A}/{\Gamma}
.

Being an affine space,
{\mathscr{A}}
is contractible, so over
\mathscr{A}
we can trivialise
the determinant line bundle and hence identify the effective action functional
Z
with
a complex function. However, this might not be possible over the orbit
space
\mathscr{A}/{\Gamma}
: if the descended line bundle
is non-trivial then we cannot identify the effective action functional with a complex
function in a gauge-invariant way, i.e. the gauge symmetry is anomalous.
The line bundle over
\mathscr{A}/{\Gamma}
is trivial if
and only if we can choose a
{\Gamma}
-equivariant
trivialisation
of the line
Z(A)=\det({D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A})
. By our general discussion above, the obstruction to this is an element of

{\rm H}^{1}\big({\Gamma};{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{\mathscr{A}}\big)
, where
{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{\mathscr{A}}
is the diffeological space of maps from
{\mathscr{A}}
to
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
.
An explicit formula for this smooth 1-cocycle is obtained in
[
CM95
]
.

7.2
Odd dimensions: The Faddeev-Mickelsson-Shatashvili anomaly

We shall now generalise the construction from the Section
7.1

to bundle gerbes. For this, we need to introduce a categorification of
the first group cohomology which takes values in a smooth abelian
2-group.
We use a definition along the lines of
[
BMS19
]
,
adjusted to the smooth setting.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
{\mathsf{A}}
a smooth abelian 2-group equipped
with a left action
\rho
of
\underline{G}\,
.
A
smooth higher 1-cocycle
on

G
with values in
{\mathsf{A}}
consists of

•

a morphism
\lambda\colon\underline{G}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{A}}
,

g\longmapsto\lambda_{g}
,
in
\mathscr{H}
, and

•

a smooth natural isomorphism
\chi_{g,g^{\prime}}\colon\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}(\lambda_{g^{\prime}})\longrightarrow\lambda_{g\,g^{\prime}}
of smooth functors
\underline{G}\times_{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}\underline{G}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{A}}
,

such that for every
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
:

•

\lambda_{e_{c}}=I_{c}
where
I_{c}
is the monoidal identity object of the fibre
{\mathsf{A}}_{|c}
of
{\mathsf{A}}
over
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
,
and where
e_{c}\colon c\longrightarrow G
is the constant map at the identity element of
G
,

•

\chi_{e_{c},\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,}
and
\chi_{\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,,e_{c}}
agree with the left and right unitor morphisms
in
{\mathsf{A}}_{|c}
, and

•

the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}\big(\lambda_{g^{\prime}}\otimes\rho_{g^{\prime}}(\lambda_{g^{\prime\prime}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}(\lambda_{g^{\prime}\,g^{\prime\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g\,g^{\prime}\,g^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}(\lambda_{g^{\prime}})\otimes\rho_{g\,g^{\prime}}(\lambda_{g^{\prime\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g\,g^{\prime}}\otimes\rho_{g\,g^{\prime}}(\lambda_{g^{\prime\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\rho_{g}(\chi_{g,g^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{g,g^{\prime}\,g^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{g,g^{\prime}}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{g\,g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}}}

(7.5)

commutes for all
g,g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\in G(c)
.

We will also need the concept of a higher coboundary.

Definition 7.6
.

Let
(\lambda,\chi)
and
(\lambda^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
be higher 1-cocycles
on a Lie group
G
valued in a smooth abelian 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
. A
higher coboundary
between
(\lambda,\chi)
and
(\lambda^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})

consists of

•

a morphism
\theta\colon*\longrightarrow{\mathsf{A}}
, and

•

smooth isomorphisms
\omega_{g}\colon\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}(\theta)\longrightarrow\theta\otimes\lambda^{\prime}_{g}
for every
g\in\underline{G}\,
,

such that
\omega_{e_{c}}
agrees with the symmetry isomorphism

\beta_{\mathsf{A}}
, and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}\big(\lambda_{g^{\prime}}\otimes\rho_{g^{\prime}}(\theta)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}(\theta)\otimes\rho_{g}(\lambda^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\otimes\lambda^{\prime}_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}(\lambda^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g}\otimes\rho_{g}(\lambda_{g^{\prime}})\otimes\rho_{g\,g^{\prime}}(\theta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g\,g^{\prime}}\otimes\rho_{g\,g^{\prime}}(\theta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\otimes\lambda^{\prime}_{g\,g^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{g}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{g,g^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{g,g^{\prime}}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{g\,g^{\prime}}}

(7.7)

commutes for all
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
and
g,g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\in G(c)
.

Remark 7.8
.

There is a natural definition of morphisms between higher
coboundaries, but these are not relevant for our purposes.

\vartriangleleft

Let
G
be a connected Lie group,
M
a manifold with smooth
G
-action
\Phiit\colon G\times M\longrightarrow M
, and

(\mathcal{G},A,\chi)
a
G
-equivariant bundle gerbe on
M
.
The equivariant structure on
\mathcal{G}
can be described by a splitting

s_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\underline{G}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
, as
explained in
Section
5.6
. Assume furthermore that
\mathcal{G}
is trivial as bundle gerbe, i.e. there exists a
1-isomorphism

E\colon\mathcal{I}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}
.
From the results in Section
5.6
we can deduce that
the obstruction to the existence of an equivariant structure on

E
is the higher 1-cocycle

\displaystyle(f\colon c\longrightarrow G)\longmapsto s_{\mathcal{G}}(f)^{-1}\circ\widehat{E}\big(s_{\mathcal{I}}(f)\big)\ \in\ \mathrm{BGrb}(c\times M)(\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G},\operatorname{pr}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{G})\cong\mathrm{HLBdl}(c\times M)\ .

(7.9)

This 1-cocycle is trivial precisely if there exists a natural isomorphism to
the constant 1-cocycle at the trivial line bundle
I
. The choice of such an
isomorphism corresponds to the equivariant structure on
E
.
The isomorphisms
\chi
in Definition
7.4
use the smooth 2-group
structure on
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
, and the isomorphisms which are part of the
splittings
s_{\mathcal{G}}
and
s_{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\widehat{E}
.
Generally, two 1-isomorphisms
\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of bundle gerbes differ
by the action of a 1-automorphism of
\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
. Hence the obstruction to the existence
of an equivariant isomorphism is an element of the first higher group cohomology of
G
with coefficients
in the smooth abelian 2-group
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
.

Let us now explain the relation to the Hamiltonian description of chiral anomalies
in terms of bundle gerbes, which was worked out in
[
Mic85
,
CM95
,
CM96
,
CMM97
,
CMM00
]
.
Let
M
be a based odd-dimensional compact Riemannian spin manifold,
9
9

9

The type of Hamiltonian anomaly discussed here can only occur on odd-dimensional manifolds, since otherwise
the chirality operator could be used to define consistent polarisations.

P
a principal
G
-bundle on
M
, and
\rho\colon G\longrightarrow\mathsf{End}(V)

a representation of
G
describing the matter content of the gauge theory. Again
we denote by
\mathscr{A}
the affine space of connections on
P
and by
\Gamma

the pointed group of gauge transformations. For every
A\in\mathscr{A}
we can construct a massless
Dirac operator

{D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A}\colon\Gamma(M;S\otimes V)\longrightarrow\Gamma(M;S\otimes V)\ ,

where
S\longrightarrow M
is the spinor bundle. The Dirac operator is a first
order self-adjoint elliptic differential operator, which serves as the
first quantised Hamiltonian acting on the
one-particle Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}=\Gamma(M;S\otimes V)
.

To define the fermionic Fock space
\mathcal{F}_{A}(\mathcal{H})
of the quantum field theory in the presence of a gauge field

A\in\mathscr{A}
, one has to pick a polarisation
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{+}(A)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{-}(A)
.
In general there are gauge fields
A\in\mathscr{A}
for which the
Dirac operator
{D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A}
has zero modes; for these fields there is no
universal and natural way of choosing such a polarisation.
Denote by
\mathscr{A}_{0}\subset\mathscr{A}\times\mathbb{R}
the subset of pairs
(A,r)
such
that the real number
r
is not contained in the spectrum of
{D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A}
. To equip this space with a diffeology
we use the discrete diffeology on
\mathbb{R}
. For every point
(A,r)\in\mathscr{A}_{0}

we get a decomposition of the one-particle Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{+}(A,r)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{-}(A,r)

into the positive and negative eigenstates of the operator
{D\hskip-7.11317pt/\penalty}{}_{A}-r\,1_{\mathcal{H}}
.
The corresponding Fock bundle
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{H})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
has fibres

\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{H})_{|(A,r)}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}\,\mathcal{H}_{+}(A,r)\otimes\text{\Large$\wedge$}\,\mathcal{H}_{-}(A,r)^{\vee}\ .

It is shown in
[
CM96
]

that the corresponding projective Hilbert bundle descends to a bundle over
\mathscr{A}
,
and hence it induces a bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathscr{A}
.
The bundle gerbe can be explicitly described in terms of determinant lines associated to families of Dirac operators, see
[
CM96
, Section 5]
for details.
Since

{\mathscr{A}}
is contractible, over
{\mathscr{A}}
the
projective Hilbert bundle is trivial and hence is associated to a bundle of Hilbert
spaces.
Again the action of
\Gamma
on
{\mathscr{A}}
lifts to an equivariant
structure on
\mathcal{G}
. Therefore
\mathcal{G}
as well as the projective
Hilbert bundle descends to the orbit space
{\mathscr{A}}/{\Gamma}
. The Faddeev-Mickelsson-Shatashvili anomaly is the obstruction to the existence of a well-defined bundle of
Hilbert spaces over
{\mathscr{A}}/{\Gamma}
, i.e. to the existence of a trivialisation of the descended projective Hilbert bundle.
Equivalently, the anomaly vanishes if and only if
\mathcal{G}
descends to a
trivial bundle gerbe on
{\mathscr{A}}/{\Gamma}
.
This in turn is the case if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is trivial as a

\Gamma
-equivariant
bundle gerbe on
{\mathscr{A}}
.

From the general discussion above it follows that
the obstruction to the equivariant triviality of
\mathcal{G}
is a smooth
higher 1-cocycle on
\Gamma
with
values in
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{\mathscr{A}}
. Because
{\mathscr{A}}
is contractible, there is an
equivalence

\mathrm{HLBdl}^{\mathscr{A}}\cong*\big/\hskip-4.26773pt\big/{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{\mathscr{A}}

with the smooth category with one object and the diffeological mapping space
{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{\mathscr{A}}
as morphisms. Since this is a smooth 2-group
with one object, Definition
7.4
in this instance is equivalent to the definition
of an ordinary group 2-cocycle on
\Gamma
with values in
{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{\mathscr{A}}
. That the obstruction to
the vanishing of the anomaly is a 2-cocycle of this form is well-known,
see e.g.
[
CM96
]
; this cocycle reproduces the usual Schwinger terms in the commutator anomaly for
the gauge group action.
What is new here is the construction of a
smooth higher
1-cocycle, which only reduces to an ordinary 2-cocycle because the space
\mathscr{A}
is
contractible, as well as a rigorous incorporation of the smooth structures. Computing this cocycle explicitly
and comparing it to the Faddeev-Mickelsson-Shatashvili cocycle is beyond the scope of this paper. We expect this to be
possible using index theory following
[
CMM97
]
.

8
Application III: The string group

Any compact simple Lie group
G
has homotopy groups
\pi_{3}(G)\cong\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
\pi_{i}(G)\cong\mathrm{H}^{i}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong 0
for
i=0,1,2
; that is,
G
is 2-connected.
It is of interest in topology and geometry (see
e.g.
[
DHH11
,
Sto96
,
ST04
]
),
as well as in string theory (see e.g.
[
SS20
]
), to study 3-connected approximations to
G
that arise as group extensions of
G
.
We denote such approximating objects by
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
and call them models
for the
string group
of
G
.
There is a variety of interpretations of what this means, based on different choices of ambient higher categories in which one considers
G
to be a group object.
The general theme, however, is that one needs a way to realise a generator of
\pi_{3}(G)\cong\mathbb{Z}
geometrically in the chosen framework, and a string group model for
G
will generally be a choice of such a generator.

In this final section we recall the definition and construction of a
topological string group model, and show that our extensions

{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
from Section
3
provide a smooth enhancement thereof. We then propose the smooth
2-groups
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
from Section
5
as new string group models, for the specific choices of

M=G
and of a gerbe
\mathcal{G}
on
G
whose Dixmier-Douady class
generates
\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. A model for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
which is
very similar in spirit to our model
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
was found in
[
FRS16
]
.
However, that construction relies on the choice of connection on
\mathcal{G}
and considers connection-preserving symmetries of
\mathcal{G}
.
Here, in contrast, we exhibit a construction of string group models for
G
from symmetries of gerbes on
G
without connections, and thus from representatives of the third integer cohomology of
G
rather than its differential cohomology.
We defer further details and comments to Section
8.2
.

8.1
A smooth string group model

The simplest and original framework for considering string group models is that of topological spaces.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
G
be a compact simple simply-connected Lie group.
A topological model for the
string group

{\mathrm{String}}(G)
of
G
is
a topological 3-connected group
{\mathrm{String}}_{\mathtt{t}}(G)
along with a fibration

{\mathrm{String}}_{\mathtt{t}}(G)\longrightarrow G
whose typical fibre is an Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(\mathbb{Z};2)
.

Using homotopy and cohomology groups one shows that
{\mathrm{String}}_{\mathtt{t}}(G)
cannot be a finite-dimen-sional Lie group
[
NSW13
]
.
If a topological string group model can be enhanced to consist of
smooth spaces (such as Fréchet manifolds or diffeological spaces),
we denote it by
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
and refer to it as a smooth model for the string group of
G
.

We recall Stolz’ model as a topological group
[
Sto96
]
:
let
{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
denote the projective unitary group of an infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert space.
As a consequence of Kuiper’s Theorem,
{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
has homotopy type
K(\mathbb{Z};2)
.
Hence the classifying space
{\mathsf{B}}{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
has homotopy type
K(\mathbb{Z};3)
, while at the same time it classifies topological principal
{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
-bundles.
In particular, isomorphism classes of
{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
-bundles on a space
X
are
in one-to-one correspondence with elements of the set
\mathrm{H}^{3}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Let
P\longrightarrow G
be a principal
{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
-bundle on
G
such that
P

corresponds to a generator of
\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
; such
\mathbb{P}{\operatorname{U}}
-bundles on
G
are called
basic
.
Let
\widehat{G}
denote the group of
{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
-equivariant homeomorphisms of
P
to itself which act on
G
as left multiplication by some element of
G
.
We can topologise
\widehat{G}
as a subgroup of the topological group of homeomorphisms
P\longrightarrow P
.
Thus
\widehat{G}
comes with a continuous surjective group homomorphism
\widehat{G}\longrightarrow G
.
The gauge group
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
is the subgroup of
\widehat{G}
of those
elements whose projection to
G
is the identity element
e\in G
.

Theorem 8.2

(
[
Sto96
, Section 5]
and
[
NSW13
]
)
.

The extension of topological groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

(8.3)

exhibits
\widehat{G}
as a topological model for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
.

The crux of the proof of this theorem is showing that
{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)
is
homotopy equivalent to
{\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
, i.e. that it is an Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(\mathbb{Z};2)
.
Part of the content in
[
NSW13
]
is to enhance this topological string group model to a smooth model in the sense that the groups appearing are Fréchet Lie groups.

The group extension
\widehat{G}\longrightarrow G
agrees with the extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)\longrightarrow G

constructed in Section
3
when we set
M=G
and
H={\mathbb{P}\mathrm{U}}
,
and let
G
act on itself by left multiplication.
Thus we immediately obtain

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
P\longrightarrow G
be a basic
\mathbb{P}{\operatorname{U}}
-bundle.
The extension of diffeological groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gau}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

(8.5)

exhibits
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
as a smooth model for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
.

8.2
Smooth string 2-group models

Let
G
be a compact simply-connected Lie group, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a
bundle gerbe on
G
whose Dixmier-Douady class generates

\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})
; such a bundle gerbe is said to be
basic
.
Let
\Phiit\colon G\times G\longrightarrow G
denote the left action of
G
on itself by left multiplication.
In the spirit of Section
5
, it is reasonable to expect that we should also be able to interpret the smooth 2-groups
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
as models for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
.
The idea of constructing
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
as a smooth 2-group has also
been considered in e.g.
[
BCSS07
,
SP11
,
Wal12a
,
NSW13
,
FRS16
]
.
In the remainder of this section we will describe how
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
can be seen as a string 2-group model.
By Theorem
5.96
it then follows that
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
is also a model for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
.

Smooth string 2-group models usually consist of extensions of
G
by
the smooth 2-group
{\mathsf{B}}{\operatorname{U}}(1)
, the delooping of the smooth abelian group
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
.
However, recall that in Theorem
5.63
we
established
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
as an extension of
\underline{G}
by the smooth
abelian 2-group
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{G}
.
Our point here is that what matters for string group models is only the homotopy type of the fibre and the total space of the map
{\mathrm{String}}(G)\longrightarrow G
, so that there is a lot of flexibility in choosing the smooth 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
that extends
G
.
Observe that this ambiguity is inherent already in Definition
8.1
.
This forces us to state which smooth 2-groups
{\mathsf{A}}
are admissible in order to obtain smooth 2-group extensions of
G
that deserve to be called string group models.
Our proposed definition for smooth string 2-group models emphasises
the structure of
{\mathsf{A}}
as a smooth analogue of an Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(\mathbb{Z};2)
.
Note that for every smooth abelian 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
and any manifold
M
, we can
define Čech cohomology of
M
with coefficients in
{\mathsf{A}}
by evaluating (a delooping of
{\mathsf{A}}
) on the Čech nerve of good open coverings of
M
.

The definition of a smooth string 2-group model is thus a two-step process:
we first fix the homotopy type of the extending 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
in a
geometric way, and then we have to make precise when an
{\mathsf{A}}
-extension of
G
has the correct homotopy type.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
H
be a diffeological group.
The
delooping

{\mathsf{B}}\underline{H}\in\mathscr{H}
is the category fibred in groupoids
over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
whose objects are the Cartesian spaces
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, and whose morphisms
c_{0}\longrightarrow c_{1}
are pairs
(f_{01},h_{01})
of smooth maps
f_{01}\colon c_{0}\longrightarrow c_{1}
and
h_{01}\colon c_{0}\longrightarrow H
.
Composition of morphisms is given by
(f_{12},h_{12})\circ(f_{01},h_{01})=\big(f_{12}\circ f_{01},h_{01}\,(h_{12}\circ f_{01})\big)
.

If
H
is abelian, then
{\mathsf{B}}\underline{H}
naturally becomes a smooth
abelian 2-group.

Definition 8.7
.

A smooth 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
is
string-admissible
if it is abelian
and equivalent (as a smooth 2-group) to the delooping
{\mathsf{B}}\underline{H}
of a
diffeological abelian group
H
whose underlying topological space is
an Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(\mathbb{Z};2)
.

From the equivalence
{\mathsf{A}}\simeq{\mathsf{B}}\underline{H}\,
it follows that Čech cohomology with coefficients in
{\mathsf{A}}
is equivalent to Čech cohomology with coefficients in
H
, shifted by one degree.
Then since
H
has homotopy type
K(\mathbb{Z};2)
, it follows that there are isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(M;{\mathsf{A}})\cong{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(M;{\mathsf{B}}\underline{H})\cong\big[M,{\mathsf{B}}^{k+1}H\big]\cong\big[M,K(\mathbb{Z};k+2)\big]\cong\mathrm{H}{}^{k+2}(M;\mathbb{Z})

(8.8)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
(For the notion of Čech cohomology with coefficients in higher smooth groups, we refer the reader to
[
Sch13
,
NSS15
]
.)

From any smooth principal 2-bundle
{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow\underline{M}
over a manifold
M
with
structure 2-group
{\mathsf{A}}
, we can distil a Čech cohomology class as
follows:
let
{\mathcal{U}}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a good open covering of
M
. Denote
intersections by
U_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}:=U_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap U_{i_{n}}
.
Viewing the (intersections of) open patches
U_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}\hookrightarrow M
as objects in
\underline{M}\,
, we denote by
{\mathsf{P}}_{|U_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}
the fibres of
{\mathsf{P}}
over these objects.
By Definition
5.40
, we can choose an object
\psi_{i}\in{\mathsf{P}}_{|U_{i}}
for every
i\in I
.
We can further choose an object
{\mathsf{a}}_{ij}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{|U_{ij}}
for
every
i,j\in I
and an isomorphism
g_{ij}\colon\psi_{i|U_{ij}}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}_{ij}\longrightarrow\psi_{j|U_{ij}}
in
{\mathsf{P}}_{|U_{ij}}
(where we
have chosen pullbacks of
\psi_{i}
and
\psi_{j}
to
{\mathsf{P}}_{|U_{ij}}
).
Over the triple overlaps
U_{ijk}
we obtain isomorphisms

\beta_{ijk}\colon\psi_{i|U_{ijk}}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}_{ij|U_{ijk}}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}_{jk|U_{ijk}}\longrightarrow\psi_{i|U_{ijk}}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}_{ik|U_{ijk}}\ ,

(8.9)

which are uniquely determined by the properties of the Grothendieck
fibration
{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow\underline{M}\,
(as previously, since
\underline{M}
has discrete fibres, it follows that
{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\underline{M}}^{\mathtt{h}}{\mathsf{P}}={\mathsf{P}}\times_{\underline{M}}{\mathsf{P}}
).
Since the morphisms
(1_{\psi_{i|U_{ijk}}},\beta_{ijk})
lie in the image of the action functor
{\mathsf{P}}\times{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\underline{M}}{\mathsf{P}}
, there are unique isomorphisms

\alpha_{ijk}\colon{\mathsf{a}}_{ij|U_{ijk}}\otimes_{\mathsf{A}}{\mathsf{a}}_{jk|U_{ijk}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{a}}_{ik|U_{ijk}}

(8.10)

in
{\mathsf{A}}_{|U_{ijk}}
, which satisfy the required coherence condition over quadruple overlaps by the fact that they are constructed as Cartesian lifts of identity morphisms.
Hence these data assemble into an
{\mathsf{A}}
-valued Čech 1-cocycle on

M
with respect to the open covering
{\mathcal{U}}
.
One can check that other choices of coverings and sections lead to 1-cocycles that become equivalent to
({\mathsf{a}}_{ij},\alpha_{ijk})
when passing to a common refinement of good open coverings.

Definition 8.11
.

Let
G
be a compact simply-connected Lie group, and let
{\mathsf{A}}
be a string-admissible smooth 2-group.
A
smooth 2-group model
for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
is a smooth 2-group extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{String}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

(8.12)

such that the principal 2-bundle
{\mathrm{String}}(G)\longrightarrow\underline{G}
represents a generator of
\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
under the isomorphism
{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{1}(G;{\mathsf{A}})\cong\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})
.

With these definitions we have

Theorem 8.13
.

For any 2-connected manifold
M
, the smooth 2-group
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
is string-admissible.

Theorem 8.14
.

Let
G
be a compact simply-connected Lie group, and let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{BGrb}(G)
be a basic bundle gerbe.
Let
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
be the smooth 2-group extensions of
\underline{G}
by
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{G}
constructed from
\mathcal{G}
with respect to the left action of
G
on itself by left multiplication.
Then both
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
and
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
are smooth 2-group models for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
in the sense of Definition
8.11
.

Remark 8.15
.

Note that there is a model for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
based on regression and transgression of the basic gerbe on
G

[
Wal12a
]
.
Similarly, our model for
{\mathrm{String}}(G)
which we obtain from
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{\mathsf{L}}
also heavily relies on the transgression-regression formalism.
However, the resulting models are very different:
Waldorf’s model in
[
Wal12a
]

is
the regression of the transgression of the basic gerbe.
Waldorf observes that this gerbe picks up a multiplicative structure upon application of
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{T}
.
Since any gerbe on a manifold can be seen as an extension of
M
by
{\mathrm{B}}{\operatorname{U}}(1)
as Lie groupoids, the resulting gerbe provides a string group model in the sense of
[
SP11
]
.
Our construction differs from this significantly not only in the fact that we work with smooth 2-groups instead of Lie 2-groups; most notably, we obtain an extension of
G
by the much larger smooth 2-group
{\mathrm{B}}(\underline{{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{H}})
, which is not equivalent to
{\mathrm{B}}\underline{{\operatorname{U}}(1)}
as a smooth 2-group, but only on the level of their underlying homotopy types (see Theorem
8.13
as well as
[
Bun20b
]
).

\vartriangleleft

The rest of this section is devoted to the proofs of Theorems
8.13
and
8.14
.
We begin with a few results that will combine to prove that
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
is string-admissible.
Then we will prove Theorem
8.14
by
observing that the
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{G}
-valued Čech 1-cocycles we obtain
from the 2-bundle
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})
agree with those obtained from local
trivialisations of the bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
M
be a simply-connected manifold and
x\in M
a fixed base point.
Then the evaluation map
{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}\colon{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)
,
g\longmapsto g(x)
and the inclusion
{\mathsf{c}}\colon{\operatorname{U}}(1)\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}
as constant maps form a homotopy equivalence of diffeological spaces.
10
10

10

See Example
2.6
for the definition of the diffeological mapping space.

Proof.

Since
{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}\circ{\mathsf{c}}=1_{{\operatorname{U}}(1)}
, it is enough to construct a smooth homotopy
{\mathsf{c}}\circ{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}\cong 1_{{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}}
.
Let
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)
,
r\longmapsto\exp(2\pi\,\mathrm{i}\,r)
be the universal cover of
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
.
The assumption that
M
is simply-connected implies (see e.g.
[
Bre93
, Theorem 4.1]
) that
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{U}}(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(8.17)

admits a unique continuous lift
\widehat{f}\colon M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
for arbitrary
f\in{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}
after fixing the lift at one point. We verify that the map
\widehat{f}
is smooth. Fix a point
y\in M
and a sufficiently small
open neighbourhood
U_{y}
of
y
which we identify with an open subset
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d=\dim(M)
.
The restriction of
f
to
U_{y}
is a plot of
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
.
Hence by Proposition
2.11
it admits a smooth lift
\widehat{f}_{y}\colon U_{y}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
for sufficiently small

U_{y}
. From the uniqueness statement for lifts we obtain
\widehat{f}_{|U_{y}}=\widehat{f}_{y}+r_{y}
for a fixed integer

r_{y}\in\mathbb{Z}
. This implies that
\widehat{f}
is smooth and hence
shows that the map
\pi^{M}:\mathbb{R}^{M}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}

is surjective.

Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{U}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{\mathsf{c}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{ev}}}_{x}}

(8.18)

where
\widehat{{\mathsf{c}}}\,(r)(y)=r
for all
y\in M
, and where

\widehat{{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}}(g^{\prime})=g^{\prime}(x)
for all
g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
.
We define a homotopy
\widehat{h}\colon 1_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\longrightarrow\widehat{{\mathsf{c}}}\circ\widehat{{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}}
by setting
\widehat{h}_{t}(g^{\prime})(y)=g^{\prime}(y)\,(1-t)+g^{\prime}(x)\,t
.
This homotopy descends to the desired homotopy
h\colon 1_{{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{c}}\circ{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}
.

We verify that the homotopy
h
is smooth: let
n
be a natural number,
c\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
a Cartesian space and
f\colon c\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}\times[0,1]
a plot; that is, the maps

\operatorname{pr}_{[0,1]}\circ f\colon c\longrightarrow[0,1]
and
(\operatorname{pr}_{{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}}\circ f)^{\dashv}\colon c\times M\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)
are smooth.
We have to show
that
h\circ f\colon c\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}
is a plot. By the arguments above we can lift
(\operatorname{pr}_{{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}}\circ f)^{\dashv}

to a smooth map
c\times M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
because
c\times M
is simply-connected. This implies that we can lift
f
to a smooth map
\widehat{f}\colon c\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}\times[0,1]
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{M}\times[0,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}\times[0,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{M}\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

(8.19)

commute. The result now follows since
\widehat{h}
and the projection
\pi^{M}:\mathbb{R}^{M}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}
are smooth.
∎

Lemma 8.20
.

There is an inclusion
{\mathsf{B}}(\underline{{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
of smooth 2-groups which is given by sending
c\in{\mathsf{B}}(\underline{{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}})
(cf. Definition
8.6
) to the trivial line bundle on
c\times M
.
If
\mathrm{H}^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z})=0
, then this inclusion is an equivalence.

Proof.

We readily see that the inclusion respects the group structures, and that it is fully faithful.
If
\mathrm{H}^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z})=0
, then
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}{}_{|c}\cong\mathrm{HLBdl}(c\times M)
is connected, so that in this case the inclusion is also fully faithful on all fibres.
Thus it is an equivalence on every fibre and hence an equivalence in
the 2-category
\mathscr{H}
by
[
Vis05
, Proposition 3.36]
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
8.16
and
8.20
, we conclude that
\mathrm{HLBdl}^{M}
is string-admissible for any 2-connected manifold
M
; that is, we have proven Theorem
8.13
.

For the diffeological group
H={\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}
and a 2-connected manifold

M
, there is an explicit isomorphism
{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M})\cong{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;{\operatorname{U}}(1))
for
k>0
, for any manifold
X
, given by

Proposition 8.21
.

Let
M
be a 2-connected manifold with a fixed base point
x\in M
.
For any manifold
X
, evaluation at
x\in M
induces an isomorphism

{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M})\cong{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;{\operatorname{U}}(1))
for
k>0
of Čech cohomology
groups with coefficients in the sheaves of smooth
{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}
-valued and
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
-valued functions, respectively.

Proof.

Consider the sequence of diffeological groups

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M}\longrightarrow 1\ ,

(8.22)

which is exact by the argument from the proof of Lemma
8.16
.
The sheaf
\mathbb{R}^{M}
admits a partition of unity by picking a partition of unity for the
sheaf of smooth
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions and a constant extension to
\mathbb{R}^{M}
-valued functions; hence
{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;\mathbb{R}^{M})=0
for any manifold
X
and for any
k\geq 1
.
Now the statement follows from applying the five lemma to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k+1}(X;\mathbb{R}^{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k+1}(X;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;\mathbb{R}^{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k+1}(X;\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k+1}(X;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;{\operatorname{U}}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{k}(X;\mathbb{Z})}

(8.23)

induced by the long exact sequence in sheaf cohomology and the evaluation at
x\in M
.
∎

It remains to determine the Čech cohomology class in
{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{1}(G;\mathrm{HLBdl}^{G})\cong\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
determined by the extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\underline{G}\,
.
The isomorphisms from Lemma
8.20
and
Proposition
8.21
are useful in achieving
this.
From the smooth 2-group extension
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\underline{G}
we can
extract a Čech 2-cocycle on
G
with values in the sheaf of smooth
{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{G}
-valued
functions.
To construct it, we first follow the procedure of the paragraph
preceding Definition
8.11
to extract

\mathrm{HLBdl}^{G}
-valued cocycle data and then choose local trivialisations of the line bundles which comprise it (which amounts to choosing an inverse for the equivalence from Lemma
8.20
).

Let
{\mathcal{U}}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a good open cover
of
G
, let
\pi_{i}\colon U_{i}\times G\longrightarrow G
denote the projection
onto the second factor, and let
m_{i}\colon U_{i}\times G\longrightarrow G
be the
multiplication map restricted to
U_{i}\times G
. We choose and fix 1-isomorphisms

\psi_{i}\colon m_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\pi_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}

along with
adjoint inverses

\psi_{i}^{-1}
, which induce
equivalences
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\mathcal{G})_{|U_{i}}\cong\mathrm{HLBdl}(U_{i}\times G)
of groupoids.

On double intersections
U_{ij}
we can form the automorphism
\psi_{ij}\coloneqq\psi^{-1}_{j|U_{ij}}\circ\psi_{i|U_{ij}}
of
m_{ij}^{*}\mathcal{G}
.
The isomorphism
\psi_{ij}
can be identified with a line bundle
L_{ij}
on

U_{ij}\times G
.
Since
\mathrm{H}^{2}(G;\mathbb{Z})=0
, we can choose and fix a trivialisation of
L_{ij}
for each
i,j\in I
.

On triple intersections
U_{ijk}
we get a 2-isomorphism

\psi_{ijk}\colon\psi_{jk|U_{ijk}}\circ\psi_{ij|U_{ijk}}\longrightarrow\psi_{ik|U_{ijk}}
inducing an isomorphism

L_{jk}\otimes L_{ij}\longrightarrow L_{ik}
of line bundles over

{U_{ijk}}\times G
.
(In contrast to the construction in the paragraph above Definition
8.11
, here the isomorphisms
\psi_{ijk}
can be obtained directly from the choice of
\psi_{ij}
.)
Using the trivialisations of these line bundles
we obtain a smooth map
{U_{ijk}}\times G\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)
or equivalently
a map
c_{ijk}\colon U_{ijk}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{G}
. The collection
c_{ijk}
form
a smooth
{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{G}
-valued Čech 2-cocycle.

The corresponding cohomology class is independent of all choices involved:
let
\psi_{i}^{\prime}\colon m_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\pi_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}
be a different
set of 1-isomorphisms. The automorphism
\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\psi_{i}^{\prime}
of

m_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}
can be identified
with a line bundle
{\Lambda}_{i}
over
U_{i}\times G
. The definition of
L_{ij}

implies
L_{ij}\otimes{\Lambda}_{i}\cong{\Lambda}_{j}\otimes L^{\prime}_{ij}
.
We can pick once and for all trivialisations of all bundles involved to identify this
morphism with a function
A_{ij}\colon U_{ij}\times G\longrightarrow{\operatorname{U}}(1)
.
The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{ij}\otimes{\Lambda}_{i}\otimes L_{jk}\otimes{\Lambda}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda}_{j}\otimes L_{ik}\otimes{\Lambda}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda}_{j}\otimes{\Lambda}_{k}\otimes L^{\prime}_{ij}\otimes L^{\prime}_{jk}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda}_{j}\otimes{\Lambda}_{k}\otimes L^{\prime}_{ik}}

(8.24)

commutes over
U_{ijk}
, which follows from the fact that all inverses were chosen
to be adjoint so that the corresponding diagram involving

\psi_{i}
and
\psi_{i}^{\prime}
commutes.
Applying the trivialisations we get

c_{ijk}\,A_{ik}=c^{\prime}_{ijk}\,A_{ij}\,A_{jk}\ .

(8.25)

This argument also shows that the cocycles define the same cohomology class if
\psi_{i}^{\prime}=\psi_{i}

and only the trivialisations of
L_{ij}
differ.

The image of
c_{ijk}
in
{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{2}(G;{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{G})
under the isomorphism

{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{2}(G;{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{G})\cong{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{2}(G;{\operatorname{U}}(1))
of Proposition
8.21

can be computed by restricting each
\psi_{i}
to
U_{i}\times\{e\}\subset U_{i}\times G
; the restriction
\psi_{|U_{i}\times\{e\}}
is a 1-isomorphism
\mathcal{G}_{|U_{i}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}|_{e}
. After fixing once and for all a trivialisation of
\mathcal{G}_{|e}
, this is just a trivialisation of
\mathcal{G}_{|U_{i}}
. This shows that the
image of
c_{ijk}
in
{\check{\mathrm{H}}}{}^{2}(G;{\operatorname{U}}(1))\cong\mathrm{H}^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})
agrees with the cocycle
c_{\mathcal{G}}

classifying the bundle gerbe
\mathcal{G}
, which proves Theorem
8.14
.

Remark 8.26
.

The arguments involving cocycles can be adjusted to the simpler case
of principal bundles over the Lie group
G
. In that case, starting from a principal
{\operatorname{U}}(1)
-bundle
P
on
G
we
get a principal
{\operatorname{U}}(1)^{G}
-bundle
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P)
on
G
which is homotopy equivalent
to
P
.
The homotopy equivalence is induced by the maps
{\mathrm{ev}}_{x}
and
{\mathsf{c}}
from Lemma
8.16
.
We can iterate the procedure to get larger and larger groups
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(\cdots{\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}({\mathrm{Sym}}_{G}(P))\cdots)
.
However, these groups are all topologically equivalent,
so that iterating the procedure does not produce anything that is topologically novel.

\vartriangleleft

Appendix A
Properties of smooth principal 2-bundles

A.1
Surjectivity on objects and homotopy pullbacks

Here we provide some technical background on smooth groupoids, as introduced in Definition
5.12
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\pi\colon{\mathsf{X}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
be objects in
\mathscr{H}
.

(1)

Either
{\mathsf{X}}=\varnothing
or
\pi
is surjective on objects.

(2)

Let
p\colon{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}
be a morphism in
\mathscr{H}
whose
underlying functor is an essentially surjective Grothendieck fibration.
Then
p
is surjective on objects.

Proof.

To see (1), observe that
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
has a terminal object
*\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.
Thus since
\pi
is a Grothendieck fibration, if
{\mathsf{X}}_{|*}=\pi^{-1}(*)
is non-empty then so is
{\mathsf{X}}_{|c}
for any
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.
For any
c\in{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
there exists a morphism
x\colon*\longrightarrow c
in
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
given by choosing any point
x\in c
.
It follows that as soon as
{\mathsf{X}}\neq\varnothing
, it has only non-empty fibres over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
.

Claim (2) follows from the general observation that a Grothendieck
fibration is essentially surjective if and only if it is surjective on objects.
∎

We now consider the setup of Definition
5.34
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category, let
\pi_{i}\colon{\mathsf{D}}_{i}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
, for
i=0,1
, and
\pi_{\mathsf{E}}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}
be Grothendieck fibrations in groupoids, and let
F_{i}\colon{\mathsf{D}}_{i}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{E}}
, for
i=0,1
, be morphisms of categories fibred in groupoids over
\mathscr{C}
.

(1)

({\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1},\pi_{\tt h})\in\mathscr{H}
,
i.e.
\pi_{\tt h}
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids.

(2)

Any morphism
G=(G_{0},G_{\mathsf{E}},G_{1})
of diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\mathsf{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}_{1}}

(A.3)

in
\mathscr{H}
, where all vertical morphisms are equivalences, induces an
equivalence
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{D}}^{\prime}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}^{\prime}_{1}
.

(3)

If
F_{1}
(resp.
F_{0}
) is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids,
then the inclusion
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
is an equivalence, and the
projections
\operatorname{pr}_{0}^{\tt h}\colon{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{D}}_{0}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{0}\colon{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{D}}_{0}
(resp. the projections
\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{\tt h}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{1}
to
{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
) are Grothendieck fibrations in groupoids.

Proof.

To prove (1), we first show that every morphism in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
is
\pi_{\tt h}
-Cartesian.
Consider morphisms
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\colon(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})\longrightarrow(d^{\prime\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime}_{1})
and
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})\colon(d_{0},\eta,d_{1})\longrightarrow(d^{\prime\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime}_{1})
in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
.
By assumption
\pi_{0}(d_{0})=\pi_{1}(d_{1})
in
\mathscr{C}
(and analogously for
d^{\prime}_{i},d^{\prime\prime}_{i}
), and
\pi_{0}(\psi_{0})=\pi_{1}(\psi_{1})
(and analogously for
\varphi_{i}
).
Let
\chi\colon\pi_{0}(d_{0})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(d^{\prime}_{0})
be a morphism in
\mathscr{C}
such that
\pi_{0}(\psi_{0})\circ\chi=\pi_{0}(\varphi_{0})
.
Since
\pi_{\mathsf{E}}
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, there exists a unique lift
\chi_{{\mathsf{E}},i}\colon F_{i}(d_{i})\longrightarrow F_{i}(d^{\prime}_{i})
induced by each of the pairs
(F_{i}(\psi_{i}),F_{i}(\varphi_{i}))
of morphisms in
{\mathsf{E}}
.
Similarly, the pairs
(\psi_{i},\varphi_{i})
induce unique lifts

\chi_{i}:d_{i}\longrightarrow d_{i}^{\prime}
of
\chi
to
{\mathsf{D}}_{i}
along
\pi_{i}
.
The uniqueness of lifts (along
\pi_{\mathsf{E}}
) implies that
F_{i}(\chi_{i})=\chi_{{\mathsf{E}},i}
for
i=0,1
.
It remains to show that
(\chi_{0},\chi_{1})
defines a morphism
(d_{0},\eta,d_{1})\longrightarrow(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})
.
That is, we need to prove that
F_{1}(\chi_{1})\circ\eta=\eta^{\prime}\circ F_{0}(\chi_{0})
in
{\mathsf{E}}
.
So far we have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(d_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(d^{\prime}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(d^{\prime\prime}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(d_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(d^{\prime}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(d^{\prime\prime}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(\chi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(\varphi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}(\psi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(\chi_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(\varphi_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(\psi_{1})}

(A.4)

in
{\mathsf{E}}
, where each face of this diagram commutes, apart from the back left square.
The commutativity of that square is what we need to prove.
For this, observe that both
\eta^{\prime}\circ F_{0}(\chi_{0})
and
\eta\circ F_{1}(\chi_{1})
provide lifts of
\chi
to
{\mathsf{E}}
with respect to the morphisms
\eta^{\prime\prime}\circ F_{0}(\varphi_{0})\colon F_{0}(d_{0})\longrightarrow F_{1}(d^{\prime\prime}_{1})
and
F_{1}(\psi_{1})\colon F_{1}(d^{\prime}_{1})\longrightarrow F_{1}(d^{\prime\prime}_{1})
.
The desired identity now follows from the uniqueness of such lifts along the functor
\pi_{\mathsf{E}}
.

Next we need to show that for any morphism
f\colon c\longrightarrow c^{\prime}
in

\mathscr{C}
and any object
(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})
in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
over
c^{\prime}
, there exists a lift

\widehat{f}=(f_{0},f_{1})
of
f
to
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
with codomain
(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})
.
Such a lift is obtained by lifting
f
to morphisms
f_{i}\colon d_{i}\longrightarrow d^{\prime}_{i}
in
{\mathsf{D}}_{i}
using the fact that
\pi_{i}
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, for
i=0,1
.
An isomorphism
\eta\colon F_{0}(d_{0})\longrightarrow F_{1}(d_{1})
compatible with

f_{0},f_{1}
is obtained by filling the horn given by the morphisms

\eta^{\prime}\circ F_{0}(f_{0})
and
F_{1}(f_{1})
over the identity morphism
1_{c}

in
\mathscr{C}
. The filler is an isomorphism since the fibre
{\mathsf{E}}_{|c}

is a groupoid.

To prove (2), we note that
by
[
Vis05
, Proposition 3.36]
the induced morphism
G_{0}\times_{G_{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}G_{1}
is an equivalence in
\mathscr{H}
if and only
if it restricts to an equivalence of groupoids between all fibres of

\pi_{\tt h}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{\tt h}
.
A direct inspection on any
c\in\mathscr{C}
reveals that

\pi_{\tt h}^{-1}(c)=\pi_{0}^{-1}(c)\times_{\pi_{\mathsf{E}}^{-1}(c)}^{\tt h}\pi_{1}^{-1}(c)

(A.5)

as groupoids, and it is well-known that equivalences of spans of groupoids induce equivalences on homotopy pullbacks of groupoids.

To prove (3), we first show that
\operatorname{pr}_{0}^{\tt h}
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids.
Consider a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d^{\prime\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0},\eta,d_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})}

(A.6)

in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
, and a morphism
\chi_{0}\colon d_{0}\longrightarrow d^{\prime}_{0}
in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}
such that
\psi_{0}\circ\chi_{0}=\varphi_{0}
.
We obtain a commutative triangle in
{\mathsf{E}}
formed by the morphisms
F_{1}(\psi_{1})
,
F_{1}(\varphi_{1})
, and
\eta^{\prime}\circ F_{0}(\chi_{0})\circ\eta^{-1}
.
Since
F_{1}
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, this gives rise
to a unique lift
\chi_{1}:d_{1}\longrightarrow d_{1}^{\prime}
of the latter morphism to
{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
.
The pair
(\chi_{0},\chi_{1})
is automatically a morphism in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
which projects to
\chi_{0}
.
If
(\chi^{\prime}_{0},\chi^{\prime}_{1})
were any other such filling over
\chi_{0}
of the horn given by
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
and
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
, it would immediately follow that
\chi_{0}^{\prime}=\chi_{0}
, and the uniqueness of fillings of
\psi_{1},\varphi_{1}
over
\eta^{\prime}\circ F_{0}(\chi_{0})\circ\eta^{-1}
would imply that
\chi_{1}^{\prime}=\chi_{1}
.

Let
\varphi_{0}\colon d_{0}\longrightarrow d^{\prime}_{0}
be a morphism in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}
, and let

(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})
be an object in
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
which projects to
d^{\prime}_{0}
.
Let
\varphi_{1}
be a lift along
\pi_{1}
of
f\coloneqq\pi_{0}(\varphi_{0})
with codomain
d^{\prime}_{1}
.
The pair of morphisms
(\eta^{\prime}\circ F_{0}(\psi_{0}),F_{1}(\psi_{1}))
then gives rise to a cospan in
{\mathsf{E}}
.
Both morphisms project to
f
in
\mathscr{C}
and hence, since
\pi_{\mathsf{E}}

is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, there exists a unique
isomorphism
\eta\colon F_{0}(d_{0})\longrightarrow F_{1}(d_{1})
such that
(d_{0},\eta,d_{1})\in{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
and such that
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
is a lift of
\psi_{0}
to
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
with codomain
(d^{\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{1})
.
The claim for
\operatorname{pr}_{0}
is proven in an entirely analogous way by
restricting
\eta
,
\eta^{\prime}
and
\eta^{\prime\prime}
to be identity morphisms.

Finally, consider the inclusion functor
{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{D}}_{0}\times_{{\mathsf{E}}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{D}}_{1}
.
Since
\operatorname{pr}_{0}^{\tt h}
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, so is its restriction to each fibre over
\mathscr{C}
.
It is well-known that the inclusion of a pullback of groupoids into the homotopy pullback is an equivalence in case one of the functors in the diagram is a Grothendieck fibration.
Thus our inclusion functor is an equivalence on each fibre over
\mathscr{C}
, whence the result follows by
[
Vis05
, Proposition 3.36]
.
∎

A.2
Relation to principal
\infty
-bundles

Our notion of smooth principal 2-bundle does not have any notion of ‘local triviality’ built into it.
This differs from the version of a principal 2-bundle defined in
[
SP11
]
, but is very much in the spirit of the definition of a principal
\infty
-bundle from
[
NSS15
]
.
The fact that we require essential surjectivity is our version of saying that the (homotopy) fibres of the bundle should be non-empty.
In contrast to
[
NSS15
]
we have to require fibration properties because we do not work purely in an
\infty
-categorical framework.
We shall now show that an
{\mathsf{H}}
-principal 2-bundle in
\mathscr{H}
in the sense of Definition
5.40
gives rise to a principal 2-bundle in the sense of
[
NSS15
, Definition 3.4]
, adapted from a general
\infty
-topos (described e.g. by presheaves of
\infty
-groupoids) to our situation involving presheaves of groupoids.
Let
p\colon{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}
be a morphism in
\mathscr{H}
, and let
{\mathsf{P}}^{[\bullet]}
be the Čech nerve of
p
.
We write
\operatorname{hocolim}^{\mathscr{C}}
(resp.
\operatorname{holim}^{\mathscr{C}}
) for a homotopy
colimit (resp. limit) taken in a simplicial model category
\mathscr{C}
.

Proposition A.7
.

Every morphism
p\colon{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}
in
\mathscr{H}
whose underlying functor is an essentially surjective Grothendieck fibration in groupoids gives rise to an effective epimorphism: the morphism

\underset{\Delta^{\rm op}}{\operatorname{hocolim}}^{\mathscr{H}}\,{\mathsf{P}}^{[\bullet]}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}

(A.8)

from its Čech nerve to
{\mathsf{X}}
is an equivalence.

Because of Lemma
A.2
and the assumption that
p
is a
Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, it does not matter here if one uses
the coherent Čech nerve, formed using
{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\mathsf{X}}^{\tt h}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{X}}^{\tt h}{\mathsf{P}}
, or the strict Čech nerve, formed using
{\mathsf{P}}\times_{\mathsf{X}}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{X}}{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

We work with Hollander’s model structure on
\mathscr{H}

[
Hol08
]
.
In this picture,
\mathscr{H}
is a model category enriched, tensored and cotensored in the model category
{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
(seen as a strict category).
In both
\mathscr{H}
and
{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
all objects are fibrant, and the functor
\underline{\mathscr{H}}\colon\mathscr{H}^{\text{op}}\times\mathscr{H}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
is homotopical by
[
Vis05
, Proposition 3.35]
, i.e. it preserves weak equivalences in each argument.
The enrichment of
\mathscr{H}
in
{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
even enhances to an enrichment over
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}_{\hskip-1.13791pt\Delta}}
, the category of simplicial sets with the Kan-Quillen model structure.
Thus homotopy (co)limits in
\mathscr{H}
can be computed using (co)bar constructions
[
Rie14
]
.
Let
Q
denote a cofibrant replacement functor in
\mathscr{H}
, and let
{\mathsf{Z}}\in\mathscr{H}
be an arbitrary object.
Then

\displaystyle\underline{\mathscr{H}}\Big(\underset{\Delta^{\rm op}}{\operatorname{hocolim}}^{\mathscr{H}}\,{\mathsf{P}}^{[\bullet]}\,,\,{\mathsf{Z}}\Big)\cong\underset{\Delta}{\operatorname{holim}}^{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}\ \underline{\mathscr{H}}\big(Q({\mathsf{P}}^{[\bullet]}),\,{\mathsf{Z}}\big)\cong\underset{\Delta}{\operatorname{holim}}^{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}\ \underline{\mathscr{H}}\big({\mathsf{P}}^{[\bullet]},\,{\mathsf{Z}}\big)\ ,

(A.9)

where the first equivalence stems from the fact that
{\mathsf{Z}}
is fibrant and
\mathscr{H}
is a
{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}
-enriched model category, and the second equivalence stems from the fact that
\underline{\mathscr{H}}
is homotopical.
It thus suffices to prove that the functor

p^{*}\colon\underline{\mathscr{H}}({\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{Z}})\longrightarrow\underset{\Delta}{\operatorname{holim}}^{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}}\ \underline{\mathscr{H}}\big({\mathsf{P}}^{[\bullet]},\,{\mathsf{Z}}\big)\eqqcolon{\mathfrak{Des}}_{p}({\mathsf{Z}})

(A.10)

is an equivalence of groupoids.

An object in
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{p}({\mathsf{Z}})
is a pair
(G,\eta)
of a functor
G\colon{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{Z}}
of categories fibred in groupoids over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, together with a natural isomorphism
\eta_{|(p_{0},p_{1})}\colon G(p_{0})\longrightarrow G(p_{1})
from
d_{1}^{*}G
to
d_{0}^{*}G
of functors over
{\operatorname{\mathscr{C}art}}
, where
d_{i}
are the face maps in the simplicial object
{\mathsf{P}}^{[\bullet]}
.
This natural isomorphism is subject to the conditions
d_{2}^{*}\eta\circ d_{0}^{*}\eta=d_{1}^{*}\eta
over
{\mathsf{P}}^{[3]}
and
{\Delta}^{*}\eta=1_{G}

over
{\mathsf{P}}
, where
{\Delta}:{\mathsf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{P}}^{[2]}
is the diagonal map.
A morphism
(G,\eta)\longrightarrow(G^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
in
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{p}({\mathsf{Z}})
is a natural isomorphism
\gamma\colon G\longrightarrow G^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{H}
such that
\eta^{\prime}\circ d_{1}^{*}\gamma=d_{0}^{*}\gamma\circ\eta
.

We first show that
p^{*}
is essentially surjective:
let
(G,\eta)\in{\mathfrak{Des}}_{p}({\mathsf{Z}})
be any object.
We define a functor
F\colon{\mathsf{X}}\longrightarrow{\mathsf{Z}}
as follows:
first, recalling that
p
is surjective on objects by
Lemma
A.1
, we choose a section
s\colon{\tt ob}({\mathsf{X}})\longrightarrow{\tt ob}({\mathsf{P}})
of the map of objects defined by
p
.
Then we set
F(x)\coloneqq G(s(x))\in{\mathsf{Z}}
for
x\in{\mathsf{X}}
.
Now consider a morphism
\psi\colon x\longrightarrow y
in
{\mathsf{X}}
.
Since
p
is a Grothendieck fibration,
\psi
has a lift

\widehat{\psi}\colon\widehat{x}\longrightarrow s(y)
to a morphism in
{\mathsf{P}}

with codomain
s(y)
, where
p(\widehat{x})=x
.
Define
F(\psi)\colon F(x)\longrightarrow F(y)
via the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x)=G\big(s(x)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\big(s(y)\big)=F(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\widehat{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{|(s(x),\widehat{x})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\widehat{\psi}\,)}

(A.11)

The naturality of
\eta
, together with the two conditions it
satisfies and the fact that
p
is a Grothendieck fibration in groupoids, imply that
F
is a well-defined functor.
Furthermore,
\eta
establishes an isomorphism
p^{*}F=(F,1_{F})\longrightarrow(G,\eta)
in
{\mathfrak{Des}}_{p}({\mathsf{Z}})
.
Thus
p^{*}
is essentially surjective.

That
p^{*}
is fully faithful follows from its explicit construction and the fact that
p
is essentially surjective.
∎
